# The Geometry and Dynamics of Spiral Minimal Products

**arXiv ID**: 2608.02370v1
**Authors**: Haizhong Li, Yongsheng Zhang
**Published**: 2026-08-03
**Categories**: math.DG, math.DS
**Comments**: 54 pages, 1 figure. This version supersedes and substantially strengthens arXiv:2306.03328 and arXiv:2409.19848 with new understandings and results, and also corrects some typos and inaccuracies in the earlier works
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.02370v1

## Abstract

We study spiral products $G_γ(t,x,y)=(z_1(t)f_1(x),z_2(t)f_2(y))$, formed from spherical $\mathscr{C}$-totally real immersions $f_i:M_i^{k_i}\to S^{2n_i+1}$, with $k_1+k_2>0$, and a profile $γ=(z_1,z_2)$ in $S^3$. The product is minimal precisely when the factors are minimal and $γ$, after reparametrization, is a geodesic of $\bar g=|z_1|^{2k_1}|z_2|^{2k_2}g_{S^3}$ wherever $\bar g$ is positive definite. The profile flow is Liouville integrable. On each regular two-turning component of the doubly spiral parameter domain, the complete-cell phase map is real analytic with open dense full-rank locus; hence ordinary-closing profiles of arbitrarily large primitive order are dense. At fixed regular nonzero momentum, an exact Routh completion reduces the profile Hessian to a scalar Sturm form plus two nonnegative squares. For compact minimal inputs and an ordinarily closed profile of primitive order $m_γ$, $G_γ$ satisfies $\operatorname{Ind}(G_γ)\geq\operatorname{Ind}(f_1)+\operatorname{Ind}(f_2)+2m_γ-3$.   At the contact momentum level, factor-adapted selection produces, from any prescribed pair of compact connected embedded special Legendrians, compact embedded special Legendrian products of every sufficiently large prime closing order. Their second fundamental forms are uniformly bounded, while volume and normal Morse index grow at least linearly with the order. Real spherical minimal embeddings yield analogous families. Applied to finite-holonomy horizontal lifts, it yields Delaunay-type minimal Lagrangian immersions in complex projective spaces. For canonical lifts of compact embedded inputs and a regular ordinarily closed contact profile, the primitive spherical quotient is embedded; its Hopf quotient is embedded exactly when the reduced relative winding number is $1$ and satisfies an additive Hamiltonian-index bound.

## Introduction

A spiral product couples two spherical immersions through a profile curve in𝕊3\mathbb{S}^{3}.
For minimal𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally real inputs,
minimality reduces to a weighted geodesic equation,
while ordinary return,
quotient return,
Morse index,
and embeddedness remain distinct global problems.
Theorems A–C treat the reduced dynamics,
dense closing,
and Routh–Sturm index growth.
On the contact slice,
Theorem D constructs compact embedded special Legendrian products in
spheres,
whereas Theorem E separates spherical from projective embeddedness and
identifies the exact obstruction after Hopf projection.

We use the following geometric terminology.
On a complex Euclidean space, we writeJ​z=i​zJz=izandω​(u,v)=⟨J​u,v⟩\omega(u,v)=\langle Ju,v\rangle;
on its unit sphere,αz​(v)=⟨J​z,v⟩\alpha_{z}(v)=\langle Jz,v\rangledenotes the standard contact form.

A smooth immersionf:Mk→𝕊2​n+1⊂ℂn+1f:M^{k}\to\mathbb{S}^{2n+1}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}is𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally realiff∗​α=0f^{*}\alpha=0, equivalently,⟨J​f,d​f​(X)⟩=0for every​X∈T​M.\langle Jf,df(X)\rangle=0\qquad\text{for every }X\in TM.(0.1)

Sinced​α=2​ωd\alpha=2\omegaon the contact distribution,
this condition also givesf∗​ω=0f^{*}\omega=0andJ​d​f​(T​M)⊂Nf​MJdf(TM)\subset N_{f}M.
In the maximal-dimensional casek=nk=n,
this is the Legendrian condition.

Throughout this paper,
all manifolds and submanifolds are smooth and without boundary.
A connected zero-dimensional factor means a point, of volume11and normal Morse index0; empty curvature terms are omitted.
Unless stated otherwise,
both factorsfi:Miki⟶𝕊2​ni+1⊂ℂni+1,i=1,2,f_{i}:M_{i}^{k_{i}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{2n_{i}+1}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n_{i}+1},\qquad i=1,2,

are spherical𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally real immersions.

mapped into the principal torus region,

## Theorem A(Weighted-geodesic characterization).

For the spherical𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally real inputsf1,f2f_{1},f_{2}above,
an immersed spiral productGγG_{\gamma}is minimal if and only if both
factors are minimal and, on every connected componentJJofγ−1​(𝕊pr3)\gamma^{-1}(\mathbb{S}^{3}_{\mathrm{pr}}), the restrictionγ|J\gamma|_{J}is an unparametrized geodesic ofg¯\bar{g}.

Aspiral minimal productis a minimal immersionGγG_{\gamma}of the form (0.4).
Accordingly, with minimal factors understood,
the unqualified termprofilewill henceforth mean a curveγ\gammasatisfying the weighted-geodesic condition of
Theorem A,
and therefore producing a spiral minimal product.
Thus, once the dimensions are fixed, any pair of minimal𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally real factors can be coupled with anyg¯\bar{g}-geodesic profile, without a homogeneity assumption.
When the first factor is a point,
the second is a standard real sphere,
andγ\gammais singly spiral with constantθ2\theta_{2},
one recovers a special case of the Hsiang–Lawson orbit-space
construction[24]and the classical rotational
ansatz studied by Otsuki[38].
More generally, every spherical minimal immersion becomes𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally real after complexifying its ambient Euclidean space,
so Theorem A may be iterated.

Castro–Urbano initiated product construction for producing special Lagrangian immersions in complex Euclidean space,
from two minimal Legendrian factors and a suitable Lagrangian surface[8].
In spheres, Castro–Li–Urbano then formulated the Legendrian construction in form (0.4) from Legendrian factors and a Legendre profile in𝕊3\mathbb{S}^{3}. They derived the profile equation
governing contact-stationary Legendrian products and isolated its
minimal, or special Legendrian, specialization; Hopf projection and
coning yielded Hamiltonian-minimal and minimal Lagrangians in complex
space forms, as well as Hamiltonian-minimal and special Lagrangian cones
in complex Euclidean space[9].
Haskins–Kapouleas subsequently developed the profile equation
into the twisted-product construction,
analyzed its closing and
obtained embedded special Legendrian examples
with equatorial factor inputs[22].
We place this contact sector inside the full two-momentum
weighted-geodesic system.
The present work adds the generic full-rank and dense-closing theorem
beyond the contact slice, the Routh–Sturm index bounds, factor-adapted
embeddedness for arbitrary prescribed embedded factors, and, after
Hopf projection, the reduced-winding criterion and Hamiltonian-index
inheritance.

The full profile-geodesic system is Liouville integrable.
Indeed,
for an affinely parametrizedg¯\bar{g}-geodesicγ\gamma, setℋ:=12​|γ˙|g¯2,μ1=ρ2​cos2⁡s​θ˙1,μ2=ρ2​sin2⁡s​θ˙2.\mathcal{H}:=\frac{1}{2}|\dot{\gamma}|_{\bar{g}}^{2},\qquad\mu_{1}=\rho^{2}\cos^{2}s\,\dot{\theta}_{1},\qquad\mu_{2}=\rho^{2}\sin^{2}s\,\dot{\theta}_{2}.

Since the geodesic Lagrangian is autonomous andθ1,θ2\theta_{1},\theta_{2}are cyclic,ℋ,μ1,μ2\mathcal{H},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}are conserved.
They Poisson commute and are generically independent.
Fixing their values reduces the magnitudessto a
one-degree-of-freedom effective-potential equation,
after which the two phases are recovered by quadrature.

## Definition 0.3(Regular oscillatory profiles and complete cells).

An affinely parametrizedg¯\bar{g}-geodesic profile isregular oscillatoryif its magnitudes​(t)s(t)is nonconstant,
oscillates between exactly two interior nondegenerate turning valuess−<s+s_{-}<s_{+},
and is analytically continued across both turns.
Acomplete cellis the oriented magnitude excursions−→s+→s−s_{-}\to s_{+}\to s_{-}.
The profile isdoubly spiralwhenμ1​μ2≠0\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\neq 0, andsingly spiralwhen exactly one ofμ1,μ2\mu_{1},\mu_{2}vanishes.

and defineC1,C2C_{1},C_{2}byC1=μ2μ1,C2=2​ℋμ12.C_{1}=\frac{\mu_{2}}{\mu_{1}},\qquad C_{2}=\frac{2\mathcal{H}}{\mu_{1}^{2}}.(0.5)

letΔi​(C1,C2)\Delta_{i}(C_{1},C_{2})be the change ofθi\theta_{i}over one complete cell and defineΠ​(C1,C2):=(Δ1​(C1,C2)2​π,Δ2​(C1,C2)2​π).\Pi(C_{1},C_{2}):=\left(\frac{\Delta_{1}(C_{1},C_{2})}{2\pi},\frac{\Delta_{2}(C_{1},C_{2})}{2\pi}\right).(0.6)

On the chartμ2≠0\mu_{2}\neq 0,
the corresponding normalization is obtained by exchanging the
two indices.
Ordinary closing aftermmcells
meansm​Π​(C1,C2)∈ℤ2m\Pi(C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2};
when such anmmexists,
the least positive one is denotedmγm_{\gamma}.

The following theorem shows that closedg¯\bar{g}-geodesic profiles

## Theorem B(Generic full rank and dense profile closing).

In either such normalized momentum chart,
letVVbe a connected regular two-turning
component contained in the doubly spiral locusμ1​μ2≠0\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\neq 0.
Ifk1+k2>0k_{1}+k_{2}>0,
thenΠ:V→ℝ2\Pi:V\to\mathbb{R}^{2}is
real analytic and the full-rank locus ofD​ΠD\Piis open and dense inVV.
Consequently,
every nonempty open subset ofVVcontains infinitely many closing
parameters with unbounded primitive ordinary closing orders.

For compact connected embedded𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally real factors,
quotient closing may occur before ordinary closing.
IfmGm_{G}is the primitive quotient closing order andΦ\Phithe
corresponding factor return, thenΦ\Phihas finite order,mγ=mG​ord⁡(Φ)m_{\gamma}=m_{G}\operatorname{ord}(\Phi), and the primitive domain is themGm_{G}-cell mapping torus with returnΦ\Phi; seeTheorem˜3.15.
Quotient closing alone does not imply embeddedness.

At fixed nonzero profile momentum, Routh reduction and Sturm
oscillation produce negative directions growing linearly with the
number of complete cells; factor directions give the remaining terms.

## Theorem C(Routh–Sturm index growth).

Assume that the factors are compact minimal𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally real
immersions.
Letγ\gammabe an ordinarily closed regular oscillatory profile with𝝁=(μ1,μ2)≠0\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\neq 0and primitive ordinary closing ordermγm_{\gamma},
and letGγG_{\gamma}denote the resulting immersion on its full ordinarymγm_{\gamma}-cell period domain.
ThenInd⁡(Gγ)≥Ind⁡(f1)+Ind⁡(f2)+2​mγ−3.\operatorname{Ind}(G_{\gamma})\geq\operatorname{Ind}(f_{1})+\operatorname{Ind}(f_{2})+2m_{\gamma}-3.Here and below,Ind⁡(fi)\operatorname{Ind}(f_{i})is the normal Morse index offif_{i}in its own block
sphere𝕊2​ni+1\mathbb{S}^{2n_{i}+1}.
If, in addition, the associated minimal spiral immersion descends aftermGm_{G}cells to a quotient immersionG¯\overline{G}on a complete-cell
mapping torus with factor returnΦ\Phi, thenInd⁡(G¯)≥max⁡{0,2​mG−3}\operatorname{Ind}(\overline{G})\geq\max\{0,2m_{G}-3\}.
IfΦ=Id\Phi=\operatorname{Id}, thenInd⁡(G¯)≥Ind⁡(f1)+Ind⁡(f2)+max⁡{0,2​mG−3}\operatorname{Ind}(\overline{G})\geq\operatorname{Ind}(f_{1})+\operatorname{Ind}(f_{2})+\max\{0,2m_{G}-3\}.

Seam-vanishing fields yield themGm_{G}-bound, whereas factor
directions obey the return-twisted seam condition and are fully
recovered whenΦ=Id\Phi=\operatorname{Id}; seeTheorems˜4.5,4.7and4.9.

Constant weighted minimal products and their Morse indices have been
studied in[48, Section 1.5, pp. 20–21]and[44,10,45].
A nonconstant profile adds dynamics and a closing seam.
Theorem C combines the fixed-momentum Sturm sector with the factor
sectors above; it does not determine the full remaining normal spectrum
or the nontrivial Floquet sectors. For background on Routh and Floquet
reduction, see[40,31,5].

The singly spiral levelC1=0C_{1}=0has an exact embeddedness
classification for compact embedded real inputs; seeTheorems˜5.2and5.3.
Whenk2>0k_{2}>0, such closings are also locally dense near its regular
part for prescribed phase-saturated factors, whose pointwise
scalar-phase incidences are all global symmetries; seeCorollary˜5.6.

On the contact sliceμ1+μ2=0\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}=0, equivalentlyC1=−1C_{1}=-1in the chartμ1≠0\mu_{1}\neq 0, the profile is
horizontal—equivalently,
Legendrian in𝕊3\mathbb{S}^{3}—so a spiral product of𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally real factors is again𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally real;
in maximal dimension,
it is Legendrian.
A connected minimal Legendrian is automatically orientable and,
after a constant phase rotation, special Legendrian[9, Lemma 2.1 and Proposition  2.2];
its cone is then special Lagrangian[19].

Special Lagrangian cones provide natural tangent-cone models for
singular fibers in the Strominger–Yau–Zaslow picture[42].
Classical embedded links include the Harvey–Lawson tori[19], Joyce’sU​(1)m−2U(1)^{m-2}-invariant examples[25], and Haskins’sS1S^{1}-invariant tori in𝕊5\mathbb{S}^{5}[20].
Heller–Pedit–Ouyang recently
proposed a way to
construct compact embedded special
Legendrian surfaces in𝕊5\mathbb{S}^{5}with unbounded genus[23].
At the immersed level, integrable systems and spectral curves yield
special Legendrian tori[32,7],
while Haskins–Kapouleas constructed higher-genus links by gluing[21].

Embeddedness is important in applications requiring regular cones or
multiplicity-one tangent cones with smooth links, including the
regularity, uniqueness, and bridge results of[26,11,13].

Example6.14exposes a genuine global obstruction:
the spiral-product construction need not preserve embeddedness,
even when both factors are embedded special Legendrians
and the contact profile is closed.
Theorem D below
shows that
this obstruction can nevertheless be overcome for every prescribed pair of factors:
a factor-adapted selection of contact profile produces embedded special Legendrian products without any additional symmetry or homogeneity assumptions.

## Theorem D(Embedded special Legendrian families from prescribed factors).

Letp=k1+1,q=k2+1p=k_{1}+1,q=k_{2}+1withp+q>2p+q>2, and letf1:L1p−1↪𝕊2​p−1,f2:L2q−1↪𝕊2​q−1f_{1}:L_{1}^{p-1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{2p-1},f_{2}:L_{2}^{q-1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{2q-1}be any fixed pair of compact connected embedded special Legendrians.
For every sufficiently large primeℓ\ell, there is a contact profile
of primitive ordinary closing orderℓ\ellwhose associated productFℓ:𝕊1×L1×L2↪𝕊2​(p+q)−1F_{\ell}:\mathbb{S}^{1}\times L_{1}\times L_{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{2(p+q)-1}is an embedded special Legendrian withInd⁡(Fℓ)≥Ind⁡(f1)+Ind⁡(f2)+2​ℓ−3\operatorname{Ind}(F_{\ell})\geq\operatorname{Ind}(f_{1})+\operatorname{Ind}(f_{2})+2\ell-3.
The resulting products have uniformly bounded second fundamental
forms and volumes comparable toℓ\ell; their unrestricted normal
Morse indices grow at least linearly with volume.
Consequently, the family contains an infinite pairwise noncongruent
subfamily.

For compact connected real spherical minimal embeddings withk1+k2>0k_{1}+k_{2}>0, complexification gives an analogous embedded minimal𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally real construction.
Here the closed profiles depend only on(k1,k2)(k_{1},k_{2})and may be
chosen in any prescribed regular contact-energy interval; seeTheorems˜5.16and5.19.

For the projective construction, letℓi:Lini↬ℂ​ℙni\ell_{i}:L_{i}^{n_{i}}\looparrowright\mathbb{CP}^{n_{i}},i=1,2i=1,2,
be connected minimal Lagrangian immersions.
By the finite-cover lifting result recalled inProposition˜6.1, each admits a finite
holonomy coverL^i→Li\widehat{L}_{i}\to L_{i}carrying a horizontal
special Legendrian liftfi:L^i↬𝕊2​ni+1f_{i}:\widehat{L}_{i}\looparrowright\mathbb{S}^{2n_{i}+1}.
Applying the contact construction above to these lifts and then the
Hopf projection givesL¯γ:ℝ×L^1×L^2⟶ℂ​ℙn1+n2+1.\overline{L}_{\gamma}:\mathbb{R}\times\widehat{L}_{1}\times\widehat{L}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{CP}^{n_{1}+n_{2}+1}.

The local product–Hopf mechanism is due to Castro–Li–Urbano.
The global quotient-domain and embeddedness results developed here,
including the exact reduced-winding criterion for compact inputs,
do
not appear to have been formulated systematically before.
The point–sphere specialization recovers Anciaux’s equivariant
construction inℂ​ℙn\mathbb{CP}^{n}[2].

For compact embedded inputs,
embeddedness separates at the spherical and projective levels.
Even whenγ\gammais ordinarily closed,
the primitive spherical and projective quotients may close beforeγ\gammacompletes its ordinary period.
ByTheorem˜6.3,
the spherical quotient in this situation is always embedded,
but Hopf projection need not preserve embeddedness:
inExample˜6.13, the
projected image has exactly2525clean self-intersection44-tori.
The reduced relative winding number gives the exact remaining
obstruction;
seePropositions˜6.4and6.5.

## Theorem E(Projective Delaunay construction).

For the connected minimal Lagrangian inputs fixed above,
every regular oscillatory contact profileγ\gammadetermines
a Delaunay-type minimal Lagrangian immersionL¯γ\overline{L}_{\gamma}inℂ​ℙn1+n2+1\mathbb{CP}^{n_{1}+n_{2}+1}.
If theℓi\ell_{i}are compact embeddings andγ\gammais
ordinarily closed, then the lifted spiral product has an embedded
primitive spherical quotient, andL¯γ\overline{L}_{\gamma}descends
to a compact minimal Lagrangian immersion.
The descended immersion is embedded precisely when the reduced
relative winding number ofγ\gammaequals11.
Under these compact embedded closing hypotheses,
if its primitive projective period containsNNcomplete cells,
then its Hamiltonian Morse index is at leastIndHam⁡(ℓ1)+IndHam⁡(ℓ2)+2​N−2\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathrm{Ham}}(\ell_{1})+\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathrm{Ham}}(\ell_{2})+2N-2.

Here “Delaunay-type” refers only to periodic neck–bulge
variation, not to the classical constant-mean-curvature
surfaces of Delaunay[12].
We writeIndHam\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathrm{Ham}}for the Hamiltonian Morse index.
Lagrangian and unrestricted normal variations are discussed
separately inRemark˜6.12.

## Organization

Sections 1–4 develop the weighted-geodesic reduction,
closing theory, and Routh–Sturm index transfer;
Sections 5–6 develop the contact constructions and analyze
embeddedness at the spherical and projective levels.
Technical calculations are collected in the appendices.
For quick reference,
the introductory theorems correspond to the body as follows:Theorem A⟷\longleftrightarrowTheorem˜1.6Theorem B⟷\longleftrightarrowTheorem˜3.9Theorem C⟷\longleftrightarrowTheorems˜4.5,4.7and4.9Theorem D⟷\longleftrightarrowTheorem˜5.18Real counterpart to D⟷\longleftrightarrowTheorem˜5.16Theorem E⟷\longleftrightarrowTheorems˜6.3,6.5and6.11

## 1.Extrinsic Geometry and the Reduced Minimality Equations

We derive the vector-valued minimality equations by separating the
factor-normal and profile-normal components.
We do not divide by either angular momentum, so the same formulas
cover both doubly and singly spiral profiles and feed directly into the
first-integral reduction of the next section.

## 1.1.The spiral ansatz and its local regimes

Retain the𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally real immersionsfif_{i}and the spiral productGγG_{\gamma}fromDefinition˜0.2.
We use the real Euclidean inner product on each ambient complex vector space.
Writegi:=fi∗​g𝕊2​ni+1g_{i}:=f_{i}^{*}g_{\mathbb{S}^{2n_{i}+1}}for the induced metrics.

## Convention 1.1(Derivative notation and affine parameters).

The weighted metricg¯\bar{g}and its affine parameter are always
indicated explicitly.
Statements over the principal torus region are understood
componentwise on its preimage underγ\gamma.

## Theorem 1.6(Weighted-geodesic characterization).

LetJJbe a connected component ofγ−1​(𝕊pr3)\gamma^{-1}(\mathbb{S}^{3}_{\mathrm{pr}}).
ThenGγ|J×M1×M2G_{\gamma}|_{J\times M_{1}\times M_{2}}is minimal if and only if
both factors are minimal andγ|J\gamma|_{J}is an unparametrized
geodesic ofg¯=ρ2​g𝕊3,ρ=ak1​bk2.\bar{g}=\rho^{2}g_{\mathbb{S}^{3}},\qquad\rho=a^{k_{1}}b^{k_{2}}.(1.10)

## Proof.

The standard conformal-change formula forg¯=ρ2​g𝕊3\bar{g}=\rho^{2}g_{\mathbb{S}^{3}}is∇Xg¯Y=∇XY+d​(log⁡ρ)​(X)​Y+d​(log⁡ρ)​(Y)​X−g𝕊3​(X,Y)​∇log⁡ρ.\nabla^{\bar{g}}_{X}Y=\nabla_{X}Y+d(\log\rho)(X)Y+d(\log\rho)(Y)X-g_{\mathbb{S}^{3}}(X,Y)\nabla\log\rho.

Here∇\nablais the round Levi–Civita connection.
For a round-unit tangentTγT_{\gamma},
its component normal to the curve is therefore(∇Tγg¯Tγ)⟂=∇TγTγ−(∇log⁡ρ)⟂.(\nabla^{\bar{g}}_{T_{\gamma}}T_{\gamma})^{\perp}=\nabla_{T_{\gamma}}T_{\gamma}-(\nabla\log\rho)^{\perp}.

The vanishing of this component is precisely the unparametrizedg¯\bar{g}-geodesic
equation, or equivalently∇Tγ𝕊3Tγ=(∇𝕊3log⁡ρ)⟂;\nabla^{\mathbb{S}^{3}}_{T_{\gamma}}T_{\gamma}=\bigl(\nabla^{\mathbb{S}^{3}}\log\rho\bigr)^{\perp};(1.11)

the tangential component only fixes the parameter.
Along the principal torus region,∇𝕊3log⁡ρ=(−k1​b/a+k2​a/b)​S\nabla^{\mathbb{S}^{3}}\log\rho=(-k_{1}b/a+k_{2}a/b)S,
so (1.11) agrees exactly with the
vanishing of the profile component in
(1.9).
The two lifted reduced factor-normal bundles are mutually orthogonal and orthogonal to
the profile-normal bundle,
independently ofΘ\Theta.
The factor-normal components vanish precisely when the two input immersions are minimal.
No division byΘ\Thetaoccurs,
so the same vector argument also covers a nonconstant
zero-angular-momentum meridian.
∎

## Remark 1.7.

Although the characterization can also be checked through
Takahashi’s classical eigenmap theorem[43,50,49],
we use the direct mean-curvature splitting because it is needed in
the subsequent curvature and index analysis.

## Remark 1.8.

The mean-curvature splitting extends continuously across every
adjacent boundary point at whichGγG_{\gamma}remains immersed.
If the profile is contained in a coordinate circle, immersion forces
the collapsed factor to be zero-dimensional, leaving the steady
great-circle case. Fixed-phase meridians are the zero-angular-momentum
profiles for which onlya,ba,bvary.

Sum the squared norms of the blocks inLemma˜1.4over the orthonormal tangent frame{E0,Eα,Fβ}\{E_{0},E_{\alpha},F_{\beta}\}.

The diagonal factor blocks contribute|B1|2a2+k1​b2a2​|S⟂|2,|B2|2b2+k2​a2b2​|S⟂|2.\frac{|B_{1}|^{2}}{a^{2}}+k_{1}\frac{b^{2}}{a^{2}}|S^{\perp}|^{2},\qquad\frac{|B_{2}|^{2}}{b^{2}}+k_{2}\frac{a^{2}}{b^{2}}|S^{\perp}|^{2}.

Since the mixed blocks occur twice in the Hilbert–Schmidt norm,
they contribute2​k1​ω12+2​k2​ω222k_{1}\omega_{1}^{2}+2k_{2}\omega_{2}^{2}.
The remaining profile block isκγ\kappa_{\gamma}.
This proves (1.12).

(1.13).

Then‖BGγ‖L∞2≤\displaystyle\|B^{G_{\gamma}}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\leq{}K12+2​k1cos2⁡s++K22+2​k2sin2⁡s−+k1​tan2⁡s++k2​cot2⁡s−+(k1​tan⁡s++k2​cot⁡s−)2.\displaystyle\frac{K_{1}^{2}+2k_{1}}{\cos^{2}s_{+}}+\frac{K_{2}^{2}+2k_{2}}{\sin^{2}s_{-}}+k_{1}\tan^{2}s_{+}+k_{2}\cot^{2}s_{-}+\bigl(k_{1}\tan s_{+}+k_{2}\cot s_{-}\bigr)^{2}.(1.14)

Consequently,
every family of regular profiles whose turning intervals remain in a fixed compact
subinterval of(0,π/2)(0,\pi/2)has uniformly bounded curvature.

## 2.Weighted Geodesics, First Integrals, and Turning Geometry

The preceding calculation identifies minimality with the weighted-geodesic equation.
In this section we integrate that equation on a monotone magnitude graph, determine the
admissible levels, and continue the local solutions analytically across their turning
points.

## 2.5.Reflection across a simple turning point

## Proposition 2.2(Geometric continuation and global magnitude oscillation).

At every simple turns0∈(0,π/2)s_{0}\in(0,\pi/2), a monotone branch has a
unique real-analytic reflected continuation in finite round arclength.
Under reflectionε,σ\varepsilon,\sigmareverse, whileσ0\sigma_{0}and the oriented phase velocities agree.
Thus every regular interior level extends for allτ∈ℝ\tau\in\mathbb{R},
with magnitude periodic betweens−s_{-}ands+s_{+}.

## Proof.

Simplicity givesD​(s)=|D′​(s0)|​|s−s0|+O​(|s−s0|2)D(s)=|D^{\prime}(s_{0})||s-s_{0}|+O(|s-s_{0}|^{2}).
Henced​τ/d​s,d​θi/d​s=O​(|s−s0|−1/2)d\tau/ds,d\theta_{i}/ds=O(|s-s_{0}|^{-1/2}),
and the branch reaches the turn in finite round arclength.
Reflect bys​(τ0+t)=s​(τ0−t),θi​(τ0+t)−θi​(τ0)=−(θi​(τ0−t)−θi​(τ0)).s(\tau_{0}+t)=s(\tau_{0}-t),\qquad\theta_{i}(\tau_{0}+t)-\theta_{i}(\tau_{0})=-\bigl(\theta_{i}(\tau_{0}-t)-\theta_{i}(\tau_{0})\bigr).

At the turn the magnitude velocity vanishes and
(2.7) gives matching phase velocities.
Thus the reflection isC1C^{1}, and analytic uniqueness for the
nondegenerate metricg¯\bar{g}gives the asserted continuation.
Iterating at both endpoints gives the global oscillation; the phase
increments govern closure.
∎

Here a collapsed boundary means that a turning value reachess=0s=0(sob=0b=0), or, after exchanging the factors,s=π/2s=\pi/2(soa=0a=0).

A positive-dimensional factor cannot collapse without destroying immersion;

## Remark 2.4(Geometric viewpoint on global spiral minimal products).

Applied to the local product pieces,
the extension theorem of Harvey and Lawson[18]gives an independent geometric construction of spiral minimal products
at the level of their images.
At each turning point ofγ\gamma,
the two adjacent branches ofGγG_{\gamma}fit togetherC1C^{1}along the turning slice;
the extension theorem identifies their union locally with a single
real-analytic minimal submanifold.
This geometric construction applies both at ordinary turning points
and in the collapsed-boundary case considered above.

## 3.Hamiltonian Reduction, Phase Maps, and Global Closing

The weighted profile equation admits a Hamiltonian formulation.
Its first integrals determine the phase-return map governing exact closing,
whereas fixed-momentum Routh reduction supplies the scalar Hessian used inSection˜4.

## 3.1.Hamiltonian interpretation, momentum map, and Routh reduction

After affine reparametrization,
the weighted-geodesic equation is the Euler–Lagrange equation of the
weighted energy LagrangianℒE\mathcal{L}_{E}defined below,
and its Legendre transform is Hamilton’s system forℋ\mathcal{H};
see[1,3].
We use the derivative convention ofConvention˜1.1.
Thusvγ:=|γ˙|𝕊3=d​τ/d​tv_{\gamma}:=|\dot{\gamma}|_{\mathbb{S}^{3}}=d\tau/dtis the round speed in the affine parameter.

## 3.1.1.Configuration space and phase symmetry

LetQ=(0,π/2)×𝕋2Q=(0,\pi/2)\times\mathbb{T}^{2},
with𝐪=(s,θ1,θ2)\mathbf{q}=(s,\theta_{1},\theta_{2}).
OnT∗​QT^{*}Q,
use the fiber coordinates(ps,μ1,μ2)(p_{s},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).
OnT∗​QT^{*}Qwe use the canonical symplectic formω=d​s∧d​ps+d​θ1∧d​μ1+d​θ2∧d​μ2.\omega=\,ds\wedge\,dp_{s}+\,d\theta_{1}\wedge\,d\mu_{1}+\,d\theta_{2}\wedge\,d\mu_{2}.

We use the conventionιXℋ​ω=d​ℋ\iota_{X_{\mathcal{H}}}\omega=d\mathcal{H}.
Since the Hamiltonian below is independent ofθ1,θ2\theta_{1},\theta_{2}, the momentaμ1,μ2\mu_{1},\mu_{2}are conserved.

## 3.1.2.Weighted length, energy, and Routh reduction

In the coordinates(s,θ1,θ2)(s,\theta_{1},\theta_{2})defineW0​(s)=ρ​(s)2,W1​(s)=ρ​(s)2​cos2⁡s,W2​(s)=ρ​(s)2​sin2⁡s,g¯=W0​d​s2+W1​d​θ12+W2​d​θ22.\begin{gathered}W_{0}(s)=\rho(s)^{2},\qquad W_{1}(s)=\rho(s)^{2}\cos^{2}s,\qquad W_{2}(s)=\rho(s)^{2}\sin^{2}s,\\
\bar{g}=W_{0}\,\,ds^{2}+W_{1}\,\,d\theta_{1}^{2}+W_{2}\,\,d\theta_{2}^{2}.\end{gathered}

Letτ\taudenote arclength for the standard round metricg𝕊3g_{\mathbb{S}^{3}}along the
profile,
and letℓg¯\ell_{\bar{g}}denote weighted arclength.
Along an affine trajectory
of energyE=ℋE=\mathcal{H},d​ℓg¯=ρ​(s)​d​τ=2​E​d​t.\,d\ell_{\bar{g}}=\rho(s)\,\,d\tau=\sqrt{2E}\,\,dt.(3.1)

Herettis the affine parameter selected by the energy formulation.
Undert↦A​t+Bt\mapsto At+B,
one hasE↦E/A2E\mapsto E/A^{2};
after fixing the energy and orientation,ttis unique up to translation and is weighted arclength whenE=12E=\tfrac{1}{2}.
Below, “round” refers tog𝕊3g_{\mathbb{S}^{3}}rather thang¯\bar{g}.
The weighted length density and weighted energy density areℒlen=W0​s˙2+W1​θ˙12+W2​θ˙22,ℒE=12​(W0​s˙2+W1​θ˙12+W2​θ˙22).\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{len}}=\sqrt{W_{0}\dot{s}^{\,2}+W_{1}\dot{\theta}_{1}^{\,2}+W_{2}\dot{\theta}_{2}^{\,2}},\qquad\mathcal{L}_{E}=\frac{1}{2}\left(W_{0}\dot{s}^{\,2}+W_{1}\dot{\theta}_{1}^{\,2}+W_{2}\dot{\theta}_{2}^{\,2}\right).

These functionals have the same nonconstant unparametrized critical curves;
the energy selects affine parameters and is used below for momenta,
Hamiltonian and Routh reduction,
and second variation.

The phase translations define an isometric𝕋2\mathbb{T}^{2}-action on(Q,g¯)(Q,\bar{g}).
The canonical momenta areps=W0​s˙,μ1=W1​θ˙1,μ2=W2​θ˙2.p_{s}=W_{0}\dot{s},\qquad\mu_{1}=W_{1}\dot{\theta}_{1},\qquad\mu_{2}=W_{2}\dot{\theta}_{2}.(3.2)

The conserved pair𝝁=(μ1,μ2)\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})is the momentum map for this action;
the same symbols denote its constant values along a trajectory.
The Legendre transform gives the standard geodesic Hamiltonianℋ​(𝐪;ps,𝝁)=12​(ps2W0+μ12W1+μ22W2).\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{q};p_{s},\boldsymbol{\mu})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{p_{s}^{2}}{W_{0}}+\frac{\mu_{1}^{2}}{W_{1}}+\frac{\mu_{2}^{2}}{W_{2}}\right).(3.3)

Hamiltonian trajectories project to affinely parametrizedg¯\bar{g}-geodesics, and unit weighted speed corresponds toℋ=12\mathcal{H}=\tfrac{1}{2}.
Sinceℋ\mathcal{H}is independent of the phase variables,{ℋ,μ1}={ℋ,μ2}={μ1,μ2}=0\{\mathcal{H},\mu_{1}\}=\{\mathcal{H},\mu_{2}\}=\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\}=0.
On the regular locusd​ℋ∧d​μ1∧d​μ2≠0d\mathcal{H}\wedge d\mu_{1}\wedge d\mu_{2}\neq 0, these three first integrals
make the three-degree-of-freedom system Liouville integrable.
Fixing𝝁\boldsymbol{\mu}leaves the one-degree-of-freedom Routh system below;
its magnitude and phase variables are reconstructed by quadratures,
although the resulting integrals need not be elementary.

On the chartμ1≠0\mu_{1}\neq 0,
use the normalized constants(C1,C2)(C_{1},C_{2})from
(0.5).
ThenC2=W0​s˙2μ12+1W1+C12W2=W0​s˙2μ12+PC1​(s).C_{2}=\frac{W_{0}\dot{s}^{\,2}}{\mu_{1}^{2}}+\frac{1}{W_{1}}+\frac{C_{1}^{2}}{W_{2}}=\frac{W_{0}\dot{s}^{\,2}}{\mu_{1}^{2}}+P_{C_{1}}(s).(3.4)

potential.
The first-order system iss˙=ε​|μ1|ρ​(s)​C2−PC1​(s),θ˙1=μ1ρ​(s)2​cos2⁡s,θ˙2=μ1​C1ρ​(s)2​sin2⁡s.\dot{s}=\frac{\varepsilon|\mu_{1}|}{\rho(s)}\sqrt{C_{2}-P_{C_{1}}(s)},\qquad\dot{\theta}_{1}=\frac{\mu_{1}}{\rho(s)^{2}\cos^{2}s},\qquad\dot{\theta}_{2}=\frac{\mu_{1}C_{1}}{\rho(s)^{2}\sin^{2}s}.(3.5)

Hereε=sgn⁡(s˙)\varepsilon=\operatorname{sgn}(\dot{s})is constant on the interior of each monotone half-cell and

Equivalently,|d​td​s|=ρ​(s)|μ1|​C2−PC1​(s).\left|\frac{\,dt}{\,ds}\right|=\frac{\rho(s)}{|\mu_{1}|\sqrt{C_{2}-P_{C_{1}}(s)}}.(3.6)

Dividing the phase equations bys˙\dot{s}recovers the magnitude-graph equations ofSection˜2.

Fixing𝝁\boldsymbol{\mu},
define thefixed-momentum effective potentialU𝝁​(s)=12​∑i=12μi2Wi​(s).U_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(s)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{\mu_{i}^{2}}{W_{i}(s)}.(3.7)

This is the angular kinetic energy required by the prescribed momentum𝝁\boldsymbol{\mu}.
The Routhian is the partial Legendre transformℛ𝝁:=(ℒE−∑i=12μi​θ˙i)|θ˙i=μi/Wi=12​W0​(s)​s˙2−U𝝁​(s).\mathscr{R}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:=\left.\left(\mathcal{L}_{E}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\mu_{i}\dot{\theta}_{i}\right)\right|_{\dot{\theta}_{i}=\mu_{i}/W_{i}}=\frac{1}{2}W_{0}(s)\dot{s}^{\,2}-U_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(s).

The subtraction of∑iμi​θ˙i\sum_{i}\mu_{i}\dot{\theta}_{i}is essential:
merely substitutingθ˙i=μi/Wi\dot{\theta}_{i}=\mu_{i}/W_{i}intoℒE\mathcal{L}_{E}gives+U𝝁+U_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}and does not yield the
fixed-momentum magnitude equation.
Its Euler–Lagrange equation is equivalent to the magnitude equation of the full weighted
geodesic system at fixed momenta.
The cyclic Euler–Lagrange equations and the reduced magnitude Euler–Lagrange equation aredd​t​(Wi​θ˙i)=0(i=1,2),W0​s¨+12​W0′​s˙2+U𝝁′​(s)=0.\frac{d}{dt}(W_{i}\dot{\theta}_{i})=0\quad(i=1,2),\qquad W_{0}\ddot{s}+\frac{1}{2}W_{0}^{\prime}\dot{s}^{2}+U_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime}(s)=0.

Thusℛ𝝁=12​W0​s˙2−U𝝁\mathscr{R}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\frac{1}{2}W_{0}\dot{s}^{2}-U_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}is the reduced Lagrangian,
whereasE𝝁:=12​W0​s˙2+U𝝁​(s)E_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:=\frac{1}{2}W_{0}\dot{s}^{2}+U_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(s)is its conserved energy.
In the normalized chart,U𝝁=12​μ12​PC1U_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}^{2}P_{C_{1}}andE𝝁=12​μ12​C2E_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}^{2}C_{2}.
The first integral determines the turning values and period quadratures.
Linearization gives the scalar Routh operator;

At the Hessian level,Theorem˜4.1identifies the elimination as an exact Schur complement:
the cyclic variables contribute two nonnegative squares,
while periodic reconstruction imposes at most two linear conditions.

## 3.2.Fixed-momentum energy levels and distinguished regimes

The geometric threshold theorem,Theorem˜2.5,
describes the admissible
parameter region.
The Hamiltonian picture separates the regular oscillatory levels from
the steady and collapsed boundary regimes.

## 3.2.1.Regular oscillatory levels

and

orbits need not close.
The time of one complete magnitude cell isTcell=2|μ1|​∫s−s+ρ​(s)C2−PC1​(s)​𝑑s=2|μ1|​∫s−s+ρ​(s)2​sin⁡s​cos⁡sDC1,C2​(s)​𝑑s.T_{\mathrm{cell}}=\frac{2}{|\mu_{1}|}\int_{s_{-}}^{s_{+}}\frac{\rho(s)}{\sqrt{C_{2}-P_{C_{1}}(s)}}\,ds=\frac{2}{|\mu_{1}|}\int_{s_{-}}^{s_{+}}\frac{\rho(s)^{2}\sin s\cos s}{\sqrt{D_{C_{1},C_{2}}(s)}}\,ds.

## Lemma 3.1(A complete cell is the primitive magnitude period).

For a regular nonsteady interior two-turning profile,
the magnitude functions​(t)s(t)has
primitive positive periodTcellT_{\mathrm{cell}}.
Consequently,{τ∈ℝ:s​(t+τ)=s​(t)​for all​t}=Tcell​ℤ.\{\tau\in\mathbb{R}:s(t+\tau)=s(t)\ \text{for all }t\}=T_{\mathrm{cell}}\mathbb{Z}.

Translatettso thats​(0)=s−s(0)=s_{-}.
The magnitude increases strictly froms−s_{-}tos+s_{+}and then decreases strictly back tos−s_{-};
hence its first positive return tos−s_{-}occurs atTcellT_{\mathrm{cell}}.
At that timeps=W0​s˙=0p_{s}=W_{0}\dot{s}=0,
so the
reduced Hamiltonian state has returned to(s−,0)(s_{-},0).

∎

## 3.2.2.Steady profiles

Suppose thatU𝝁U_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}has a strict interior minimum ats∗s_{*}.
At the minimum energyE=U𝝁​(s∗)E=U_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(s_{*}), the reduced(s,ps)(s,p_{s})-plane
consists of the single equilibrium(s∗,0)(s_{*},0).
Before quotienting the phase symmetries,
its lift is
the invariant two-torus{(s∗,θ1,θ2;0,μ1,μ2):(θ1,θ2)∈𝕋2},\left\{(s_{*},\theta_{1},\theta_{2};0,\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\in\mathbb{T}^{2}\right\},

on which the phase flow is linear.
Thus the magnitude is constant and the phases have
constant velocities.
Writingc^:=θ˙2/θ˙1\widehat{c}:=\dot{\theta}_{2}/\dot{\theta}_{1}, the equilibrium equation givesc^=C1​W1​(s∗)/W2​(s∗)=C1​cot2⁡s∗\widehat{c}=C_{1}W_{1}(s_{*})/W_{2}(s_{*})=C_{1}\cot^{2}s_{*}.
Thus steady profiles are precisely the relative equilibria of the𝕋2\mathbb{T}^{2}-action.
The
casesc^=1\widehat{c}=1andc^=−1\widehat{c}=-1differ by complex conjugation in the second block.

parameter values merely describe great circles in different positions.

computed separately because the translation fields˙\dot{s}vanishes identically.

## Proposition 3.3(Analyticity).

Fixμ1≠0\mu_{1}\neq 0and its orientation sign.
On every connected regular component with two simple interior turning points,
the turning points,
cell time,
phase increments,
and complete-cell phase map are real analytic in(C1,C2)(C_{1},C_{2}).
For any such oriented trajectory,
scaling its full initial covector byλ>0\lambda>0,
equivalently replacing(μ1,μ2,ℋ)(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mathcal{H})by(λ​μ1,λ​μ2,λ2​ℋ)(\lambda\mu_{1},\lambda\mu_{2},\lambda^{2}\mathcal{H}),
gives the same
orientedg¯\bar{g}-geodesic with a different affine parametrization.
Its phase
increments are unchanged,
while its cell time is multiplied byλ−1\lambda^{-1}.

## Proof.

The analytic implicit-function theorem[28, Theorem 2.3.5]applies toPC1​(s±)=C2P_{C_{1}}(s_{\pm})=C_{2}because∂sPC1​(s±)≠0\partial_{s}P_{C_{1}}(s_{\pm})\neq 0.
Factoring the two simple roots and using the fixed-domain substitution ofLemma˜A.1removes both endpoint square roots.
The cell-time and phase integrals are therefore real analytic in(C1,C2)(C_{1},C_{2}).
The scaling assertion follows directly from
(3.5):
multiplying the momenta byλ\lambdamultiplies every affine velocity byλ\lambda.
∎

At the finite boundaryC1=0C_{1}=0of the doubly spiral locus,Remark˜2.6applies:C1=0C_{1}=0remains a regular two-turning line whenk2>0k_{2}>0,
whereas fork2=0k_{2}=0andC2>1C_{2}>1the ordinary polar chart

torus-valued return, or equivalently the corrected real liftΠ♯\Pi^{\sharp}, across that line.
Along this collapsed line, only the endpoint(C1,C2)=(0,1)(C_{1},C_{2})=(0,1)remains singular.
The formal valueC1=∞C_{1}=\inftyis handled by exchanging the factors.

## 3.4.Ordinary closing and local resonance

On the outgoing lower-turning section,
one complete cell returns the radial state and
induces on𝕋2\mathbb{T}^{2}the rigid translation(θ1,θ2)↦(θ1+Δ1,θ2+Δ2)(mod2​π)(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\mapsto(\theta_{1}+\Delta_{1},\theta_{2}+\Delta_{2})\pmod{2\pi}.

## Definition 3.4(Ordinary return group).

For a regular oscillatory profile defineℛγ={m∈ℤ:ei​m​Δ1=1,ei​m​Δ2=1}.\mathcal{R}_{\gamma}=\{m\in\mathbb{Z}:e^{im\Delta_{1}}=1,\ e^{im\Delta_{2}}=1\}.

its positive generator is the primitive ordinary closing ordermγm_{\gamma}ofDefinition˜0.4.

## Proposition 3.5(One-cell ordinary closing is impossible).

Letp=k1+1p=k_{1}+1andq=k2+1q=k_{2}+1.
Every regular nonsteady interior two-turning profile satisfiesμ1≠0⟹0<|Δ1|<2​πp≤2​π,\mu_{1}\neq 0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 0<|\Delta_{1}|<\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{p}}\leq 2\pi,

and,
after exchanging the two factors,μ2≠0⟹0<|Δ2|<2​πq≤2​π.\mu_{2}\neq 0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 0<|\Delta_{2}|<\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{q}}\leq 2\pi.

Consequently,
every ordinarily closed regular nonsteady interior profile hasmγ≥2m_{\gamma}\geq 2.

## Theorem 3.6(Ordinary primitive closing).

A regular oscillatory profile is ordinarily closed if and only ifΠ​(C1,C2)∈ℚ2\Pi(C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}.
If, fori=1,2i=1,2,Δi/(2​π)=p^i/q^i\Delta_{i}/(2\pi)=\widehat{p}_{i}/\widehat{q}_{i}, withp^i∈ℤ\widehat{p}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z},q^i∈ℕ\widehat{q}_{i}\in\mathbb{N}, andgcd⁡(p^i,q^i)=1\gcd(\widehat{p}_{i},\widehat{q}_{i})=1,
thenmγ=lcm⁡(q^1,q^2)m_{\gamma}=\operatorname{lcm}(\widehat{q}_{1},\widehat{q}_{2}),
and the primitive ordinary period ismγ​Tcellm_{\gamma}T_{\mathrm{cell}}.
WhenΔi=0\Delta_{i}=0, this convention givesp^i/q^i=0/1\widehat{p}_{i}/\widehat{q}_{i}=0/1, so that phase imposes no
divisibility condition.

## Proof.

ByLemma˜3.1,
every profile period contains an integer numbermmof complete cells.
Aftermmcells the radial state has returned,
while the
phases have changed bym​(Δ1,Δ2)m(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}).
Hence the full Hamiltonian state
returns exactly whenm​Δi∈2​π​ℤm\Delta_{i}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z},i=1,2i=1,2.
This is possible precisely
whenΠ​(C1,C2)∈ℚ2\Pi(C_{1},C_{2})\in\mathbb{Q}^{2},
and the least suchmmislcm⁡(q^1,q^2)\operatorname{lcm}(\widehat{q}_{1},\widehat{q}_{2}).
Hamiltonian uniqueness then gives periodic repetition.
∎

## Lemma 3.7(A nonclosed regular profile is not embedded).

A maximal regular oscillatory profileγ:ℝ→𝕊3\gamma:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{S}^{3}that is not ordinarily
closed is not a topological embedding.

## Proof.

Ifγ\gammais not injective, there is nothing to prove.
We may therefore assume that it is injective.
Its one-cell translation𝚫=(Δ1,Δ2)∈𝕋2\boldsymbol{\Delta}=(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})\in\mathbb{T}^{2}has infinite order.
Compactness of𝕋2\mathbb{T}^{2}gives integersnj→∞n_{j}\to\inftywithnj​𝚫→0n_{j}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\to 0,
and the one-cell return formula yieldsγ​(t+nj​Tcell)→γ​(t)\gamma(t+n_{j}T_{\mathrm{cell}})\to\gamma(t).
Sincet+nj​Tcellt+n_{j}T_{\mathrm{cell}}escapes every compact subset ofℝ\mathbb{R},
the inverse on the
image is not continuous.
∎

## 3.4.1.Finite phase stabilizers and quotient closing

A complete-cell phase return may close the spiral product through
factor stabilizers before the profile closes ordinarily: ordinary
closing requires both phases to be11, quotient closing only
membership in the stabilizers. The primitive ordered-block quotient
below is a mapping torus.

## Definition 3.11(Phase stabilizer).

For a compact connected embeddingf:M→𝕊2​n+1f:M\to\mathbb{S}^{2n+1},
define𝖧f:={ζ∈𝕊1:ζ​f​(M)=f​(M)}.\mathsf{H}_{f}:=\{\zeta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}:\zeta f(M)=f(M)\}.(3.20)

Forζ∈𝖧f\zeta\in\mathsf{H}_{f},
embeddedness determines a uniqueφζ∈Diff⁡(M)\varphi_{\zeta}\in\operatorname{Diff}(M)byf∘φζ=ζ​ff\circ\varphi_{\zeta}=\zeta f.

## Lemma 3.12(Finite phase action).

Ifffis also𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally real,
then𝖧f\mathsf{H}_{f}is finite cyclic andζ↦φζ\zeta\mapsto\varphi_{\zeta}is a faithful isometric action on(M,gf)(M,g_{f}).

## Proof.

Compactness makes𝖧f\mathsf{H}_{f}a closed subgroup of𝕊1\mathbb{S}^{1}.
If it were the full circle,
embeddedness would makeφζ=f−1∘(ζ​f)\varphi_{\zeta}=f^{-1}\circ(\zeta f)a smooth circle action;
differentiating it would makeJ​f​(x)Jf(x)tangent tof​(M)f(M),
contrary to𝒞\mathscr{C}-total reality.
Hence it is finite cyclic.
The action law,
faithfulness,
and
isometry follow from embeddedness and ambient phase rotation.
∎

a return of the displayed ordered-block form givess​(t+τ)=s​(t)s(t+\tau)=s(t).

## 4.Second Variation and Morse Index

This section follows the chainQR⟶Qprof⟶QGQ_{R}\longrightarrow Q_{\mathrm{prof}}\longrightarrow Q_{G}.
Fixed-momentum Routh reduction first produces negative scalar profile
directions; reconstruction and normal projection transfer them toQGQ_{G}, where integrated orthogonality permits the factor-Hessian
sectors to be added.
For the normal second variation see[41],
for scalar Sturm–Liouville oscillation see[15],
for the variational Morse-index theorem see[14],
and for closed-geodesic and symplectic-path iteration see[6,27,30].
A dot retains its affine-parameter meaning.
For a minimal immersionf:Mk→𝕊Nf:M^{k}\to\mathbb{S}^{N}, our normal Jacobi
form has the sign conventionQf​(ξ)=∫M(|∇⟂ξ|2−|Aξ|2−k​|ξ|2)​d​volMQ_{f}(\xi)=\int_{M}(|\nabla^{\perp}\xi|^{2}-|A_{\xi}|^{2}-k|\xi|^{2})\,\,d\operatorname{vol}_{M}, whereAξA_{\xi}is the shape operator;Ind⁡(f)\operatorname{Ind}(f)is its maximal negative dimension.
LetIEI_{E}andILI_{L}be the index forms of
the weighted energy and length,
respectively,
letQGQ_{G}be the normal Jacobi form, and letQRQ_{R}denote the
fixed-momentum reduced Hessian.
ForV=ν​∂s+v1​∂θ1+v2​∂θ2V=\nu\,\partial_{s}+v_{1}\,\partial_{\theta_{1}}+v_{2}\,\partial_{\theta_{2}},
writeQprof​(ν,v1,v2):=IE​(V,V)Q_{\mathrm{prof}}(\nu,v_{1},v_{2}):=I_{E}(V,V).

## 4.1.Hessian reduction and periodic Morse index

Throughout this section,
the index of a quadratic form is the maximal dimension of a
negative-definite subspace;
for the compact-interval Sturm forms below,
it equals the number
of negative eigenvalues,
counted with multiplicity.
For an ordinary periodTTwe use𝒟per=Hper1​([0,T]),𝒟perprof=Hper1​([0,T];ℝ3).\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{per}}=H^{1}_{\mathrm{per}}([0,T]),\qquad\mathscr{D}_{\mathrm{per}}^{\mathrm{prof}}=H^{1}_{\mathrm{per}}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{3}).

On[0,TG][0,T_{G}], takeν∈𝒟D=H01​(0,TG)\nu\in\mathscr{D}_{D}=H^{1}_{0}(0,T_{G})and, after
cyclic reconstruction,(ν,v1,v2)∈𝒟Dprof=H01​([0,TG];ℝ3)(\nu,v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathscr{D}_{D}^{\mathrm{prof}}=H^{1}_{0}([0,T_{G}];\mathbb{R}^{3}).
The Sturm operators below are those associated with these forms on the indicatedH1H^{1}-domains.

Let𝒜R​[s]:=∫0Tℛ𝝁​(s,s˙)​𝑑t=∫0T(12​W0​(s)​s˙2−U𝝁​(s))​𝑑t\mathscr{A}_{R}[s]:=\int_{0}^{T}\mathscr{R}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(s,\dot{s})\,\,dt=\int_{0}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{2}W_{0}(s)\dot{s}^{\,2}-U_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(s)\right)\,dt

be the fixed-momentum reduced action.
Takingsε=s+ε​νs_{\varepsilon}=s+\varepsilon\nu, differentiating twice,
and integrating once by parts gives the reduced HessianQR​(ν)=[W0′​s˙​ν2]0T+∫0T(W0​ν˙2−𝒱R​ν2)​𝑑t,𝒱R:=U𝝁′′+W0′​s¨+12​W0′′​s˙2.Q_{R}(\nu)=\left[W_{0}^{\prime}\dot{s}\,\nu^{2}\right]_{0}^{T}+\int_{0}^{T}\left(W_{0}\dot{\nu}^{\,2}-\mathcal{V}_{R}\nu^{2}\right)\,dt,\qquad\mathcal{V}_{R}:=U_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime\prime}+W_{0}^{\prime}\ddot{s}+\frac{1}{2}W_{0}^{\prime\prime}\dot{s}^{\,2}.(4.1)

The displayed boundary term vanishes for Dirichlet fields and also for periodic fields,
becauses,s˙,νs,\dot{s},\nuhave matching endpoint traces.
Hence the associated
self-adjoint scalar Sturm–Liouville operator isLR​ν=−dd​t​(W0​ν˙)−(U𝝁′′+W0′​s¨+12​W0′′​s˙2)​ν.L_{R}\nu=-\frac{\,d}{\,dt}\left(W_{0}\dot{\nu}\right)-\left(U_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime\prime}+W_{0}^{\prime}\ddot{s}+\frac{1}{2}W_{0}^{\prime\prime}\dot{s}^{\,2}\right)\nu.(4.2)

The periodic operator domain has matching value and flux traces;
the Dirichlet operator
domain has zero value traces.

For the full profile Hessian,
now also takeθi,ε=θi+ε​vi+O​(ε2)\theta_{i,\varepsilon}=\theta_{i}+\varepsilon v_{i}+O(\varepsilon^{2}).

## Theorem 4.1(Routh–Hessian square completion).

Along an affinely parametrized weighted geodesic with background momenta𝛍\boldsymbol{\mu},
on either periodic or Dirichlet form domain fixed above, the full
profile Hessian for arbitrary admissible profile variations has the formQprof​(ν,v1,v2)=QR​(ν)+∑i=12∫0TWi​(v˙i+Wi′Wi​θ˙i​ν)2​𝑑t=QR​(ν)+∑i=12∫0T(δ​μi)2Wi​𝑑t,Q_{\mathrm{prof}}(\nu,v_{1},v_{2})=Q_{R}(\nu)+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}W_{i}\left(\dot{v}_{i}+\frac{W_{i}^{\prime}}{W_{i}}\dot{\theta}_{i}\nu\right)^{2}\,dt=Q_{R}(\nu)+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{(\delta\mu_{i})^{2}}{W_{i}}\,\,dt,(4.3)

whereδ​μi=Wi​v˙i+Wi′​θ˙i​ν\delta\mu_{i}=W_{i}\dot{v}_{i}+W_{i}^{\prime}\dot{\theta}_{i}\nuis the linearized angular
momentum;
thus the linearized fixed-momentum condition isδ​μi=0\delta\mu_{i}=0.
On either form domain the boundary term in (4.1) vanishes.
Thus a reduced variation lifts without additional positive cost
precisely whenv˙i=−(Wi′/Wi)​θ˙i​ν\dot{v}_{i}=-(W_{i}^{\prime}/W_{i})\dot{\theta}_{i}\nu.

## Proof.

Sinceμi=Wi​θ˙i\mu_{i}=W_{i}\dot{\theta}_{i}is constant along the background orbit,δ​μi=Wi​v˙i+Wi′​θ˙i​ν,12​(μi2Wi)′′=((Wi′)2Wi−12​Wi′′)​θ˙i2.\delta\mu_{i}=W_{i}\dot{v}_{i}+W_{i}^{\prime}\dot{\theta}_{i}\nu,\qquad\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\mu_{i}^{2}}{W_{i}}\right)^{\prime\prime}=\left(\frac{(W_{i}^{\prime})^{2}}{W_{i}}-\frac{1}{2}W_{i}^{\prime\prime}\right)\dot{\theta}_{i}^{\,2}.

Expanding the square(δ​μi)2/Wi(\delta\mu_{i})^{2}/W_{i}and using the second identity givesWi​v˙i2+2​Wi′​θ˙i​ν​v˙i+12​Wi′′​θ˙i2​ν2=(δ​μi)2Wi−12​(μi2Wi)′′​ν2.W_{i}\dot{v}_{i}^{2}+2W_{i}^{\prime}\dot{\theta}_{i}\nu\dot{v}_{i}+\frac{1}{2}W_{i}^{\prime\prime}\dot{\theta}_{i}^{2}\nu^{2}=\frac{(\delta\mu_{i})^{2}}{W_{i}}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\mu_{i}^{2}}{W_{i}}\right)^{\prime\prime}\nu^{2}.

Summing overi=1,2i=1,2,
the last terms become−U𝝁′′​ν2-U_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime\prime}\nu^{2},
becauseU𝝁=12​∑iμi2/WiU_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\mu_{i}^{2}/W_{i}.
Together with the magnitude termsW0​ν˙2+2​W0′​s˙​ν​ν˙+12​W0′′​s˙2​ν2W_{0}\dot{\nu}^{2}+2W_{0}^{\prime}\dot{s}\,\nu\dot{\nu}+\frac{1}{2}W_{0}^{\prime\prime}\dot{s}^{\,2}\nu^{2},
these terms are the second variation of the reduced action.
This proves
(4.3);
the boundary term is the one displayed in
(4.1).
∎

Locally, this reconstruction relation givesQprof=QRQ_{\mathrm{prof}}=Q_{R}without losing a negative direction;
on an ordinary period it imposes the two conditions∫0T(Wi′/Wi)​θ˙i​ν​𝑑t=0\int_{0}^{T}(W_{i}^{\prime}/W_{i})\dot{\theta}_{i}\nu\,\,dt=0,i=1,2i=1,2,
whose rank on the negative spectral space is measured below.

LetER−E_{R}^{-}denote the finite-dimensional negative spectral subspace of the periodic
operatorLRL_{R},
and definer−:=rank⁡((𝔏1,𝔏2)|ER−),𝔏i​(ν)=∫0TWi′Wi​θ˙i​ν​𝑑t.r_{-}:=\operatorname{rank}\left((\mathfrak{L}_{1},\mathfrak{L}_{2})\big|_{E_{R}^{-}}\right),\qquad\mathfrak{L}_{i}(\nu)=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{W_{i}^{\prime}}{W_{i}}\dot{\theta}_{i}\nu\,\,dt.(4.4)

Writingai=(Wi′/Wi)​θ˙ia_{i}=(W_{i}^{\prime}/W_{i})\dot{\theta}_{i}, the equationv˙i=−ai​ν\dot{v}_{i}=-a_{i}\nuhas a periodicH1H^{1}-solution if and only if𝔏i​(ν)=0\mathfrak{L}_{i}(\nu)=0; after fixing the additive constant, it gives
a bounded linear reconstruction on the closed kernel of the continuous map(𝔏1,𝔏2)(\mathfrak{L}_{1},\mathfrak{L}_{2}).
This makes the reconstruction loss canonical;
in particular it does not depend on a choice of maximal negative subspace.

By rank–nullity,
restricting a negative space to the kernel of the reconstruction map
loses exactly its rank.
Hence the square completion givesInd⁡(QR)−r−≤Ind⁡(Qprof)≤Ind⁡(QR).\operatorname{Ind}(Q_{R})-r_{-}\leq\operatorname{Ind}(Q_{\mathrm{prof}})\leq\operatorname{Ind}(Q_{R}).(4.5)

Indeed,
the left inequality follows by reconstructingER−∩ker⁡(𝔏1,𝔏2)E_{R}^{-}\cap\ker(\mathfrak{L}_{1},\mathfrak{L}_{2})with zero square cost.
For the right inequality,
projection(ν,v1,v2)↦ν(\nu,v_{1},v_{2})\mapsto\nuis injective on every negative subspace ofQprofQ_{\mathrm{prof}},
and (4.3) makes its image a negative subspace ofQRQ_{R}.
In particular,
the two periodic indices agree whenr−=0r_{-}=0;
equality is not asserted
without this condition.

For an ordinarily closed regular nonsteady interior profile with𝛍≠0\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq 0,LR​s˙=0,Ind⁡(QR)∈{2​mγ−1,2​mγ},1≤Null⁡(QR)≤2.L_{R}\dot{s}=0,\qquad\operatorname{Ind}(Q_{R})\in\{2m_{\gamma}-1,2m_{\gamma}\},\qquad 1\leq\operatorname{Null}(Q_{R})\leq 2.

The fields˙\dot{s}has two simple zeros per cell,
since at a turnW0​s¨=−U𝝁′≠0W_{0}\ddot{s}=-U_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime}\neq 0.
It therefore has2​mγ2m_{\gamma}zeros in a
primitive ordinary period.
Numbering the periodic eigenvaluesλ0,λ1,…\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\ldotsfrom zero and counting multiplicity,
periodic Sturm
oscillation places the zero eigenvalue atλ2​mγ−1\lambda_{2m_{\gamma}-1}orλ2​mγ\lambda_{2m_{\gamma}};
scalar periodic eigenvalues have multiplicity at most two.
∎

## 4.2.Normal and volume transfer

The Routh variation must first be reconstructed and only then projected to the normal
bundle.
IfV=ν​∂s+v1​∂θ1+v2​∂θ2V=\nu\,\partial_{s}+v_{1}\,\partial_{\theta_{1}}+v_{2}\,\partial_{\theta_{2}}andv˙i=−(Wi′/Wi)​θ˙i​ν\dot{v}_{i}=-(W_{i}^{\prime}/W_{i})\dot{\theta}_{i}\nu,
defineλV\lambda_{V}andV⟂V^{\perp}byλV=g¯​(V,γ˙)g¯​(γ˙,γ˙)=W0​ν​s˙+μ1​v1+μ2​v22​ℋ,V⟂=V−λV​γ˙.\lambda_{V}=\frac{\bar{g}(V,\dot{\gamma})}{\bar{g}(\dot{\gamma},\dot{\gamma})}=\frac{W_{0}\nu\dot{s}+\mu_{1}v_{1}+\mu_{2}v_{2}}{2\mathcal{H}},\qquad V^{\perp}=V-\lambda_{V}\dot{\gamma}.(4.6)

Writingν^=ν−λV​s˙\widehat{\nu}=\nu-\lambda_{V}\dot{s}andv^i=vi−λV​θ˙i\widehat{v}_{i}=v_{i}-\lambda_{V}\dot{\theta}_{i},
its lift is the explicit normal fieldLiftγ⁡(V⟂)=(ei​θ1​(−b​ν^+i​a​v^1)​f1,ei​θ2​(a​ν^+i​b​v^2)​f2).\operatorname{Lift}_{\gamma}(V^{\perp})=\Bigl(e^{i\theta_{1}}(-b\widehat{\nu}+ia\widehat{v}_{1})f_{1},\,e^{i\theta_{2}}(a\widehat{\nu}+ib\widehat{v}_{2})f_{2}\Bigr).(4.7)

Becauseg¯=ρ2​g𝕊3\bar{g}=\rho^{2}g_{\mathbb{S}^{3}},
the weighted and round projections agree.

## Proposition 4.3(Normal projection and volume transfer).

Let the factors be compact minimal𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally real immersions and letγ\gammabe a
nonconstant affine weighted geodesic in the principal torus region with𝛍≠0\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq 0.
On either
a periodic domain or a compact interval with Dirichlet boundary
conditions,
normal projection is injective on every
negative fixed-momentum profile space and preserves its negativity.
MoreoverQGγ​(Liftγ⁡(V⟂))=Vol⁡(M1)​Vol⁡(M2)v¯γ​IE​(V⟂,V⟂),v¯γ=|γ˙|g¯.Q_{G_{\gamma}}(\operatorname{Lift}_{\gamma}(V^{\perp}))=\frac{\operatorname{Vol}(M_{1})\operatorname{Vol}(M_{2})}{\bar{v}_{\gamma}}\,I_{E}(V^{\perp},V^{\perp}),\qquad\bar{v}_{\gamma}=|\dot{\gamma}|_{\bar{g}}.(4.8)

Hence every reconstructed negative Routh space transfers with the same dimension to the
normal Jacobi form of the spiral product.

## Proof.

For smooth fields on either stated domain,
writingV=V⟂+f​γ˙V=V^{\perp}+f\dot{\gamma}gives the tangential–normal splittingIE​(V,V)=IE​(V⟂,V⟂)+v¯γ2​∫|f˙|2​𝑑t,IE​(V⟂,V⟂)=v¯γ​IL​(V⟂,V⟂).I_{E}(V,V)=I_{E}(V^{\perp},V^{\perp})+\bar{v}_{\gamma}^{\,2}\int|\dot{f}|^{2}\,dt,\qquad I_{E}(V^{\perp},V^{\perp})=\bar{v}_{\gamma}I_{L}(V^{\perp},V^{\perp}).

Boundary terms cancel for periodic traces and vanish for Dirichlet traces,
so projection preserves strict negativity.
IfV⟂=0V^{\perp}=0, thenV=f​γ˙V=f\dot{\gamma}and fixed momentum givesδ​μi=μi​f˙=0\delta\mu_{i}=\mu_{i}\dot{f}=0;
since𝝁≠0\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq 0,ffis constant.
This is a zero-Hessian translation on the periodic domain and vanishes on the
Dirichlet domain, so it cannot lie nontrivially in a negative space.

For every nearby profile, the block determinant givesVol⁡(Gγε)=Vol⁡(M1)​Vol⁡(M2)​Lg¯​(γε)\operatorname{Vol}(G_{\gamma_{\varepsilon}})=\operatorname{Vol}(M_{1})\operatorname{Vol}(M_{2})L_{\bar{g}}(\gamma_{\varepsilon}).
Twice differentiating with the same boundary control yieldsQGγ=Vol⁡(M1)​Vol⁡(M2)​ILQ_{G_{\gamma}}=\operatorname{Vol}(M_{1})\operatorname{Vol}(M_{2})I_{L}on the lifted normal field,
and hence (4.8).
Orthogonal projection and the lift are smooth bundle maps along the compact principal
profile and therefore bounded on the correspondingH1H^{1}spaces;
density extends the identity from smooth fields to the stated form domains.
∎

induces the normal seam isometry𝒜Φ,z:𝒩Gγ|(TG,z)→𝒩Gγ|(0,Φ​(z))\mathcal{A}_{\Phi,z}:\mathcal{N}_{G_{\gamma}}|_{(T_{G},z)}\to\mathcal{N}_{G_{\gamma}}|_{(0,\Phi(z))}, given by𝒜Φ,z​η=η\mathcal{A}_{\Phi,z}\eta=\eta.

denote this restriction byQRD:=QR|H01​(0,TG)Q_{R}^{D}:=Q_{R}|_{H^{1}_{0}(0,T_{G})}.

## Lemma 4.4(Zero-trace fields across a mapping-torus seam).

EveryH1H^{1}normal field on the fundamental slab with zero traces at both ends belongs
to the mapping-torus form domain,
independently of the normal-bundle seam isometry𝒜Φ\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}.
Moreover,
every
finite-dimensional negative space of such fields admits,
with the same dimension and
negativity,
an arbitrarily closeH1H^{1}-approximation by smooth quotient fields supported
away from the seam.

## Proof.

SeeSection˜B.1.
∎

## Theorem 4.5(Complete-cell quotient index).

Let the factors be compact minimal𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally real immersions,
and letγ\gammabe a regular nonsteady interior profile with𝛍≠0\boldsymbol{\mu}\neq 0.
Assume thatGγG_{\gamma}admits the primitive smoothmGm_{G}-cell mapping-torus descentG¯\overline{G}described above,
with factor return mapΦ\Phi.
On the primitivemGm_{G}-cell interval,Ind⁡(QRD)=2​mG−1\operatorname{Ind}(Q_{R}^{D})=2m_{G}-1, and consequentlyInd⁡(G¯)≥max⁡{0,2​mG−3}\operatorname{Ind}(\overline{G})\geq\max\{0,2m_{G}-3\}.
Ifγ\gammais singly spiral,
only one reconstruction constraint can be nontrivial,
and the latter estimate improves toInd⁡(G¯)≥max⁡{0,2​mG−2}\operatorname{Ind}(\overline{G})\geq\max\{0,2m_{G}-2\}.

## Proof.

The interior zeros ofs˙\dot{s}are themGm_{G}upper turns and themG−1m_{G}-1intermediate lower turns.
They are simple, ands˙\dot{s}also vanishes at both endpoints.
Thus it is a Dirichlet zero-mode with exactly2​mG−12m_{G}-1interior
zeros, and Dirichlet Sturm oscillation givesInd⁡(QRD)=2​mG−1\operatorname{Ind}(Q_{R}^{D})=2m_{G}-1.

Forν∈H01​(0,TG)\nu\in H^{1}_{0}(0,T_{G}), use the background phase velocityθ˙i=μi/Wi\dot{\theta}_{i}=\mu_{i}/W_{i}and reconstruct the phase components byv˙i=−(Wi′/Wi)​θ˙i​ν\dot{v}_{i}=-(W_{i}^{\prime}/W_{i})\dot{\theta}_{i}\nuwithvi​(0)=0v_{i}(0)=0.
The endpoint conditionsvi​(TG)=0v_{i}(T_{G})=0are the two linear constraints𝔏i​(ν):=∫0TG(Wi′/Wi)​θ˙i​ν​𝑑t=0\mathfrak{L}_{i}(\nu):=\int_{0}^{T_{G}}(W_{i}^{\prime}/W_{i})\dot{\theta}_{i}\nu\,\,dt=0,i=1,2i=1,2.
Apply rank–nullity to a maximal negative Dirichlet space and the map(𝔏1,𝔏2)(\mathfrak{L}_{1},\mathfrak{L}_{2}).
The resulting negative subspace has dimension at leastmax⁡{0,(2​mG−1)−2}\max\{0,(2m_{G}-1)-2\}.
ByLemma˜4.4, its zero endpoint trace satisfies the twisted
form-domain seam condition and can be approximated by smooth quotient
fields.

ByProposition˜4.3,
normal projection injects this space into an equally dimensional

If,
for example,θ˙2≡0\dot{\theta}_{2}\equiv 0,
then𝔏2≡0\mathfrak{L}_{2}\equiv 0.
Hence the singly spiral case loses at most one dimension and gives the improved bound.
∎

## Nonclosed profiles.

LetIndc\operatorname{Ind}_{c}denote compactly supported index.
Form≥1m\geq 1, letXm=[0,m​Tcell]×M1×M2X_{m}=[0,mT_{\mathrm{cell}}]\times M_{1}\times M_{2}, with the
interval beginning and ending at lower turns, and denote its

On anmm-cell cylinder cut from any

the same Dirichlet argument givesIndD⁡(Gγ;Xm)≥2​m−3,IndD⁡(Gγ;Xm)≥2​m−2in the singly spiral case.\operatorname{Ind}_{D}(G_{\gamma};X_{m})\geq 2m-3,\qquad\operatorname{Ind}_{D}(G_{\gamma};X_{m})\geq 2m-2\quad\text{in the singly spiral case}.

Extension by zero and seam-supported smoothing therefore implyIndc⁡(Gγ)=∞\operatorname{Ind}_{c}(G_{\gamma})=\infty.

SinceVol⁡(Gγ|Xm)=m​𝒱cell\operatorname{Vol}(G_{\gamma}|_{X_{m}})=m\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{cell}},lim infm→∞IndD⁡(Gγ;Xm)Vol⁡(Gγ|Xm)≥2𝒱cell.\liminf_{m\to\infty}\frac{\operatorname{Ind}_{D}(G_{\gamma};X_{m})}{\operatorname{Vol}(G_{\gamma}|_{X_{m}})}\geq\frac{2}{\mathcal{V}_{\mathrm{cell}}}.

This statement concerns the abstract immersion and requires neither properness nor
embeddedness.

## 4.3.Input-factor sectors and quotient recovery

Factor-negative directions are first constructed on an ordinary period
and then tested for descent to the quotient.

## 4.3.1.Additive input-factor sectors

The pointwise normal-sector decomposition is not parallel,
so pointwise orthogonality alone
does not prove additivity.
Nevertheless,
the separated factor and profile variations are
orthogonal for theintegrated polarized Hessian.
The cleanest proof uses the exact
block determinant of the induced metric.

For a normal fieldξi\xi_{i}alongfif_{i},
choose a variationfi,εf_{i,\varepsilon}with normal
velocityξi\xi_{i}and define its linearized contact defect by𝒞i​ξi:=dd​ε|ε=0​fi,ε∗​α,(𝒞i​ξi)​(X)=⟨J​ξi,d​fi​(X)⟩+⟨J​fi,DX​ξi⟩.\mathscr{C}_{i}\xi_{i}:=\left.\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\right|_{\varepsilon=0}f_{i,\varepsilon}^{*}\alpha,\qquad(\mathscr{C}_{i}\xi_{i})(X)=\langle J\xi_{i},df_{i}(X)\rangle+\langle Jf_{i},D_{X}\xi_{i}\rangle.

The natural factor variational fields and their normal projections areℱ1​(ξ1)\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1}(\xi_{1})=(a​ei​θ1​ξ1,0),\displaystyle=(ae^{i\theta_{1}}\xi_{1},0),ℱ1⟂​(ξ1)\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1}^{\perp}(\xi_{1})=pr𝒩Gγ⁡ℱ1​(ξ1),\displaystyle=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathcal{N}_{G_{\gamma}}}\mathcal{F}_{1}(\xi_{1}),ℱ2​(ξ2)\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2}(\xi_{2})=(0,b​ei​θ2​ξ2),\displaystyle=(0,be^{i\theta_{2}}\xi_{2}),ℱ2⟂​(ξ2)\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2}^{\perp}(\xi_{2})=pr𝒩Gγ⁡ℱ2​(ξ2).\displaystyle=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathcal{N}_{G_{\gamma}}}\mathcal{F}_{2}(\xi_{2}).

WriteQG​(⋅,⋅)Q_{G}(\cdot,\cdot)for the polarization of the normal Jacobi form.

## Proposition 4.6(Factor-Hessian splitting).

and its image is negative definite.

proving
(4.11) and its singly spiral improvement.

## Proof.

PutΔ=Δ1>0\Delta=\Delta_{1}>0, and letA​(s)A(s)increase from0toΔ/2\Delta/2on the outgoing half-cell. The outgoing and incoming
phases at magnitudessin celljjarej​Δ+A​(s)j\Delta+A(s)and(j+1)​Δ−A​(s)(j+1)\Delta-A(s).

LetΛ=π\Lambda=\piwhenM1=−M1M_{1}=-M_{1}, andΛ=2​π\Lambda=2\piwhenM1∩(−M1)=∅M_{1}\cap(-M_{1})=\varnothing.

ofΔ\Delta, so monotonicity ofAAgives an interior
opposite-branch collision. By the orthogonal block decomposition,

These parts are independent, so the lines differ. This is impossible for a local

coincidences andℓ\ell-cell returns.
In case (i) theℓ\ell-cell return isGγ​(t+ℓ​Tcell,x,y)=Gγ​(t,−x,y)G_{\gamma}(t+\ell T_{\mathrm{cell}},x,y)=G_{\gamma}(t,-x,y); in
case (ii) it is the identity. These identities give smooth

phase values.

same image, so any compact embedded factorization identifies their
image germs. Since the second-block norm is a local profile
coordinate, slicing at fixed norm and projecting the normalized first
block identifies nonempty open germs ofM1M_{1}and−M1-M_{1}.
Real analyticity[33]and connected analytic
continuation[28, Sections 1.2 and 2.7]forceM1=−M1M_{1}=-M_{1}, a contradiction.
∎

## 5.2.1.Exact contact relation and calibrated noncrossing

Recallp=k1+1p=k_{1}+1andq=k2+1q=k_{2}+1, and introduce the phase characterχp,q:𝕋2⟶𝕊1,χp,q​(ζ1,ζ2)=ζ1p​ζ2q.\chi_{p,q}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1},\qquad\chi_{p,q}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})=\zeta_{1}^{p}\zeta_{2}^{q}.(5.4)

In maximal dimension, weighted-speed normalization and a constant
phase choice identify this reduction with the
Legendrian-product profile equations of Castro–Li–Urbano and the(p,q)(p,q)-twisted special Legendrian curve equations of Haskins–Kapouleas[9,22].
The combined-phase law controls returns, while calibration rules out
genuine self-crossings.

on every open half-cell,Ψ​(t)=Ψ0−sgn⁡(s˙​(t))​α​(s​(t)).\Psi(t)=\Psi_{0}-\operatorname{sgn}(\dot{s}(t))\,\alpha(s(t)).(5.5)

Logarithmic differentiation ofD+1=C2​cos2​p⁡s​sin2​q⁡sD+1=C_{2}\cos^{2p}s\sin^{2q}sgivesD′/(D+1)=−2​p​tan⁡s+2​q​cot⁡sD^{\prime}/(D+1)=-2p\tan s+2q\cot s,
equivalentlyp​tan⁡s−q​cot⁡s=−D′​(s)2​(D​(s)+1).p\tan s-q\cot s=-\frac{D^{\prime}(s)}{2(D(s)+1)}.(5.8)

Hence, on an outgoing half-cell,d​Ψd​s=p​tan⁡s−q​cot⁡sD​(s)=−dd​s​arctan⁡D​(s).\frac{\,d\Psi}{\,ds}=\frac{p\tan s-q\cot s}{\sqrt{D(s)}}=-\frac{\,d}{\,ds}\arctan\sqrt{D(s)}.

## Lemma 5.9(Calibrated hyperplane rigidity).

Letϕ\phibe a comass-onemm-form on a Euclidean space.
If two orientedmm-planes are calibrated byϕ\phiand share an(m−1)(m-1)-plane,
then they coincide
as oriented planes.

## Proof.

Orient the common hyperplane by a unit simple(m−1)(m-1)-vectorξ\xi,
and choose unit orthogonal
complementary vectorsv1,v2v_{1},v_{2}so thatξ∧vj\xi\wedge v_{j}gives the calibrated
orientation of thejj-th plane.
Thenϕ​(ξ∧v1)=ϕ​(ξ∧v2)=1\phi(\xi\wedge v_{1})=\phi(\xi\wedge v_{2})=1.
Ifv1≠v2v_{1}\neq v_{2},
linearity and the comass bound give1=ϕ​(ξ∧(v1+v2)/2)≤|(v1+v2)/2|<11=\phi(\xi\wedge(v_{1}+v_{2})/2)\leq\lvert(v_{1}+v_{2})/2\rvert<1,
a contradiction.
∎

## Theorem 5.10(Contact-profile noncrossing dichotomy).

Letγ:ℝ→(𝕊3)pr\gamma:\mathbb{R}\to(\mathbb{S}^{3})_{\mathrm{pr}}be a maximally extended regular nonsteady
contact profile,
taken with the affineg¯\bar{g}-parameter from
(1.10).
Every self-incidence is a genuine period:
ift1≠t2t_{1}\neq t_{2}andγ​(t1)=γ​(t2)\gamma(t_{1})=\gamma(t_{2}),
thenγ˙​(t1)=γ˙​(t2),γ​(t+t2−t1)=γ​(t)for every​t.\dot{\gamma}(t_{1})=\dot{\gamma}(t_{2}),\qquad\gamma(t+t_{2}-t_{1})=\gamma(t)\quad\text{for every }t.

Consequently exactly one of the following holds:
- (i)

γ\gammais ordinarily closed and its primitive image is an embedded circle;
- (ii)

γ\gammais injective and nonclosed,
and is not a topological embedding ofℝ\mathbb{R}into𝕊3\mathbb{S}^{3}.

## Proof.

Since the weighted profile equation depends only onk1,k2k_{1},k_{2}, we may couple the same profileγ\gammato
the standard real Legendrian factors𝕊ki⊂𝕊2​ki+1\mathbb{S}^{k_{i}}\subset\mathbb{S}^{2k_{i}+1},
replacing𝕊0\mathbb{S}^{0}by one point.
The associated product is minimal Legendrian,
and,
after a constant phase rotation,
its
cone has a fixed special-Lagrangian calibration.

At a fixed-factor self-incidence, letHHbe the common hyperplane
spanned by the outward radial direction and the two factor tangent
blocks, oriented in that order. The two calibrated cone
sheets shareHH, soLemma˜5.9identifies their positive unit
complements, hence their normalized profile directions.
Equality of the affineg¯\bar{g}-speeds therefore givesγ˙​(t1)=γ˙​(t2)\dot{\gamma}(t_{1})=\dot{\gamma}(t_{2}), and
weighted-geodesic uniqueness givesγ​(t+t2−t1)=γ​(t)\gamma(t+t_{2}-t_{1})=\gamma(t)for everytt.
The period group of a nonconstant curve is discrete;
its least positive element makes
the induced circle map injective and hence embedded,
proving (i).
If there is no self-incidence,
(ii) follows fromLemma˜3.7.
∎

For real factors, only the three nontrivial coordinate signs must be

phase-incidence relations rather than only on their stabilizers.

all nonreturn solutions inΓfi\Gamma_{f_{i}}.

The required incident phase sets are nevertheless finite.

## 6.Horizontal Lifts and Projective Delaunay Constructions

Finite horizontal lifts of minimal Lagrangian immersions provide the
inputs for the spherical theory, always on the contact levelμ1+μ2=0\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}=0. For canonical lifts, phase saturation and the
orientation character make every primitive closed spherical quotient
embedded. Hopf projection has one further obstruction, measured by
reduced relative winding.

## 6.1.Horizontal lifts and the spherical construction

Letπ:𝕊2​d+1→ℂ​ℙd\pi:\mathbb{S}^{2d+1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d}be the Hopf fibration.
We normalize the Fubini–Study metric to have holomorphic sectional
curvature44; thusRicℂ​ℙd=2​(d+1)​gFS\operatorname{Ric}_{\mathbb{CP}^{d}}=2(d+1)g_{\mathrm{FS}}.
Heretotally realmeansσ∗​ωFS=0\sigma^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}=0, equivalentlyJ​d​σ​(T​Σ)⟂d​σ​(T​Σ)Jd\sigma(T\Sigma)\perp d\sigma(T\Sigma); in symplectic
terminology this is isotropic. Every such immersion admits local
horizontal Hopf lifts, which are𝒞\mathscr{C}-totally real in the sense ofDefinition˜0.1.
For a Lagrangian immersionℓ:L→ℂ​ℙd\ell:L\to\mathbb{CP}^{d},
the pulled-back Hopf connection is flat because its curvature is a
constant multiple ofℓ∗​ωFS=0\ell^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}=0;
write its holonomy asχL:π1​(L)→𝕊1\chi_{L}:\pi_{1}(L)\to\mathbb{S}^{1}.
Horizontal lifting and its Hopf-holonomy formulation are classical;
see[39,47].
The mean-curvature–Maslov relation underlying the canonical holonomy
identity is due to Oh[37]; its Hopf formulation appears in
Wang[46].
The Maslov-level and orientation-character identities giveχL​([σ])d+1=εL​([σ])∈{±1},[σ]∈π1​(L),\chi_{L}([\sigma])^{d+1}=\varepsilon_{L}([\sigma])\in\{\pm 1\},\qquad[\sigma]\in\pi_{1}(L),

whereεL:π1​(L)→{±1}\varepsilon_{L}:\pi_{1}(L)\to\{\pm 1\}is the orientation character.
Here the covering degree ishL:=|im⁡χL|h_{L}:=|\operatorname{im}\chi_{L}|.
The following standard consequence is stated in the form needed below.

## Proposition 6.1(Finite-holonomy lift).

Letℓ:Ld↬ℂ​ℙd\ell:L^{d}\looparrowright\mathbb{CP}^{d}be a connected minimal Lagrangian immersion.
Thenim⁡χL\operatorname{im}\chi_{L}is finite cyclic of orderhL∣2​(d+1)h_{L}\mid 2(d+1),
withhL∣d+1h_{L}\mid d+1whenLLis orientable.
ThehLh_{L}-fold coverp:L^→Lp:\widehat{L}\to Lassociated withker⁡χL\ker\chi_{L}is orientable,
andℓ∘p\ell\circ phas a horizontal liftf:L^→𝕊2​d+1f:\widehat{L}\to\mathbb{S}^{2d+1},
unique up to constant phase.
After a constant phase rotation,ffis special Legendrian.
IfLLis complete
(respectively compact),
then the lifted immersion is complete
(respectively has compact domain).

## Proof.

On the universal cover choose a horizontal liftFFand a positive
orthonormal frameE1,…,EdE_{1},\ldots,E_{d}.
Minimality makes the Legendrian angleΘF=detℂ(F,F∗​E1,…,F∗​Ed)\Theta_{F}=\det_{\mathbb{C}}(F,F_{*}E_{1},\ldots,F_{*}E_{d})constant.
For a deck transformationσ\sigma, one hasF∘σ=χL​(σ)​FF\circ\sigma=\chi_{L}(\sigma)F; comparison of the two determinant
frames therefore givesχL​(σ)d+1​ΘF=εL​(σ)​ΘF,\chi_{L}(\sigma)^{d+1}\Theta_{F}=\varepsilon_{L}(\sigma)\Theta_{F},and henceχL​(σ)d+1=εL​(σ)\chi_{L}(\sigma)^{d+1}=\varepsilon_{L}(\sigma).
The orientation-character identity placesim⁡χL\operatorname{im}\chi_{L}among the2​(d+1)2(d+1)-st roots of unity
and gives the stronger divisibility in the orientable case.
Sinceker⁡χL⊂ker⁡εL\ker\chi_{L}\subset\ker\varepsilon_{L},
the associated cover is finite and orientable;
its trivial holonomy gives the required global lift.
The standard lift identities give minimality and the Legendrian
condition; a constant phase then makes the lift special.
Finite Riemannian covers preserve completeness and compactness.
∎

whereε\varepsilonis the orientation character.

## Theorem 6.3(Embedded spherical stage).

Letℓi:Lini↪ℂ​ℙni\ell_{i}:L_{i}^{n_{i}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{CP}^{n_{i}},i=1,2i=1,2,
be compact connected embedded minimal Lagrangians,
and letfi:L^i↪𝕊2​ni+1f_{i}:\widehat{L}_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{2n_{i}+1}be their canonical horizontal lifts.
Letγ\gammabe a regular,
nonsteady contact profile with nonzero momentum in the principal torus
region.

Consequently,

thenGγ:ℝ×L^1×L^2⟶𝕊2​n1+2​n2+3G_{\gamma}:\mathbb{R}\times\widehat{L}_{1}\times\widehat{L}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{2n_{1}+2n_{2}+3}

is an injective special Legendrian immersion but not a topological embedding.

then
its primitive mapping-torus quotient is a compact embedded
special Legendrian.

## Proof.

SupposeGγ​(t,x1,x2)=Gγ​(t′,x1′,x2′)G_{\gamma}(t,x_{1},x_{2})=G_{\gamma}(t^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime}),
which impliess​(t)=s​(t′),ei​(θi​(t)−θi​(t′))=hi∈𝖧fi,i=1,2.s(t)=s(t^{\prime}),\qquad e^{i(\theta_{i}(t)-\theta_{i}(t^{\prime}))}=h_{i}\in\mathsf{H}_{f_{i}},\qquad i=1,2.

Withp=n1+1p=n_{1}+1,q=n2+1q=n_{2}+1,Ψ=p​θ1+q​θ2\Psi=p\theta_{1}+q\theta_{2},h=(h1,h2)h=(h_{1},h_{2}),
andu=(ei​Δ1,ei​Δ2)u=(e^{i\Delta_{1}},e^{i\Delta_{2}}),

(5.4), givesei​(Ψ​(t)−Ψ​(t′))=χp,q​(h)∈{±1}.e^{i(\Psi(t)-\Psi(t^{\prime}))}=\chi_{p,q}(h)\in\{\pm 1\}.

Opposite interior branches are impossible,
since (5.5) would instead giveei​(Ψ​(t)−Ψ​(t′))=e±2​i​α​(s)∉{±1}e^{i(\Psi(t)-\Psi(t^{\prime}))}=e^{\pm 2i\alpha(s)}\notin\{\pm 1\}.
Since opposite branches have been excluded andssis strictly monotone
on each open half-cell,s​(t)=s​(t′)s(t)=s(t^{\prime})impliest′−t=k​Tcellt^{\prime}-t=kT_{\mathrm{cell}}for somek∈ℤk\in\mathbb{Z};
the turning-point cases follow by continuity.
Sinceθi​(t′)−θi​(t)=k​Δi\theta_{i}(t^{\prime})-\theta_{i}(t)=k\Delta_{i},
the preceding phase identities giveei​k​Δi∈𝖧fie^{ik\Delta_{i}}\in\mathsf{H}_{f_{i}},i=1,2i=1,2.
Thusk∈ℛGk\in\mathcal{R}_{G}byDefinition˜3.13.
Moreover,h=u−kh=u^{-k}, and⟨u⟩⊂ker⁡χp,q\langle u\rangle\subset\ker\chi_{p,q}by (5.6), soχp,q​(h)=1\chi_{p,q}(h)=1;
hence the return preserves the calibrated orientation.

In the absence of a positive return,GγG_{\gamma}is injective andγ\gammais not ordinarily closed.
For fixed factor points,t↦Gγ​(t,x1,x2)t\mapsto G_{\gamma}(t,x_{1},x_{2})is a linear isometric copy ofγ\gamma;
henceLemma˜3.7shows thatGγG_{\gamma}is not a topological embedding.
Otherwise,

The self-incidence statement just proved makes this immersion injective,

## 6.2.Hopf descent and projective factor slices

For the quotient-closed profiles considered below,Theorem˜6.3gives an embedded primitive spherical quotient.
Although each canonical factor lift is phase-saturated,
the resulting spherical product need not be.
As the next proposition shows,
this is precisely the obstruction to embeddedness after Hopf descent.

## Proposition 6.4(Hopf quotient and phase saturation).

LetF:Xd↪𝕊2​d+1F:X^{d}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}^{2d+1}be a compact connected Legendrian embedding,
with phase stabilizer𝖧F\mathsf{H}_{F}.
Then𝖧F\mathsf{H}_{F}is finite cyclic and acts freely onXX.
The Hopf map induces an immersionF¯:X/𝖧F→ℂ​ℙd\overline{F}:X/\mathsf{H}_{F}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d},
which is embedded if and only ifF​(X)F(X)is phase-saturated.
IfFFis minimal Legendrian,
thenF¯\overline{F}is minimal Lagrangian.

## Proof.

ByLemma˜3.12,𝖧F\mathsf{H}_{F}is finite cyclic, and its action is
free because a nontrivial scalar fixes no point of the sphere.
SinceFFis Legendrian,d​F​(T​X)dF(TX)is horizontal for the Hopf
submersion, whered​πd\piis injective; sinceX→X/𝖧FX\to X/\mathsf{H}_{F}is a local diffeomorphism,F¯\overline{F}is
an immersion. Two quotient classes have the same Hopf image precisely whenF​(y)=ζ​F​(x)F(y)=\zeta F(x)for someζ∈𝕊1\zeta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.
They represent the same quotient class exactly whenζ∈𝖧F\zeta\in\mathsf{H}_{F}. ThusF¯\overline{F}is injective if and only ifF​(X)F(X)is
phase-saturated; compactness then makes it an embedding.
The final assertion follows from the horizontal mean-curvature

## 6.3.Hamiltonian index inheritance

The block form of the Hopf output separates the two input Hamiltonian sectors from the
profile sector,
without requiring an analysis of the full normal bundle.
In this subsection,gig_{i}denotes the metric induced onLiL_{i}by the Fubini–Study metric.
Here and below,τ\taudenotes round arclength along the profile,
the unit-speed base coordinate for the induced spherical and
projective metrics.
It is distinct from the weighted-affine parameterttused in the
Hamiltonian reduction; by
(3.1),∂τ=(ρ/2​E)​∂t\partial_{\tau}=(\rho/\sqrt{2E})\partial_{t}.
Thusτ\tau, rather thantt, is the natural variable for the
Laplacian of the warped metric below.

## Lemma 6.10(Warped-product cover of the projective domain).

Under the hypotheses ofTheorem˜6.5,
suppose that the primitive projective period of𝒳γ\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}consists ofNNcomplete cells.
There is a finite regular Riemannian covering𝔓:𝒳γ⟶(𝕊T1×L1×L2,gw).\mathfrak{P}:\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}\longrightarrow\bigl(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{T}\times L_{1}\times L_{2},g_{\mathrm{w}}\bigr).

Here𝕊T1:=ℝ/T​ℤ\mathbb{S}^{1}_{T}:=\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}is the projective-domain base circle andTTis the total round length of the primitive projective period; moreover,gw=d​τ2+𝔞​(τ)​g1+𝔟​(τ)​g2,𝔞=cos2⁡s,𝔟=sin2⁡s.g_{\mathrm{w}}=\,d\tau^{2}+\mathfrak{a}(\tau)g_{1}+\mathfrak{b}(\tau)g_{2},\qquad\mathfrak{a}=\cos^{2}s,\quad\mathfrak{b}=\sin^{2}s.(6.3)

In particular, writingb1​(Y):=dimH1​(Y;ℝ)b_{1}(Y):=\dim H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{R}),b1​(𝒳γ)≥b1​(L1)+b1​(L2)+1.b_{1}(\mathcal{X}_{\gamma})\geq b_{1}(L_{1})+b_{1}(L_{2})+1.(6.4)

## Proof.

ByTheorem˜6.5(ii),
the quotient identifications preserve the profile coordinate moduloTTand act on the canonical factor covers by deck transformations.
The resulting map, its warped metric, and the transfer calculation are
proved inSection˜C.3.

## Remark 6.12(What does and does not transfer).

With the same Fubini–Study normalization,
identify a normal fieldVVwithαV=ιV​ωFS\alpha_{V}=\iota_{V}\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}.
Lagrangian fields correspond to closed one-forms and Hamiltonian fields
to exact ones. Hodge decomposition and Oh’s operatorΔ1−2​m\Delta_{1}-2mshow that every harmonic one-form contributes one
negative direction; hence[36]IndLag⁡(L¯γ)=IndHam⁡(L¯γ)+b1​(𝒳γ).\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathrm{Lag}}(\overline{L}_{\gamma})=\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathrm{Ham}}(\overline{L}_{\gamma})+b_{1}(\mathcal{X}_{\gamma}).(6.7)

Thusb1b_{1}appears only when the Hamiltonian statement is translated
to Lagrangian variations. Combining (6.4) andTheorem˜6.11givesIndLag⁡(L¯γ)≥IndLag⁡(ℓ1)+IndLag⁡(ℓ2)+2​N−1.\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathrm{Lag}}(\overline{L}_{\gamma})\geq\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathrm{Lag}}(\ell_{1})+\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathrm{Lag}}(\ell_{2})+2N-1.

AlsoInd⁡(L¯γ)≥IndLag⁡(L¯γ)\operatorname{Ind}(\overline{L}_{\gamma})\geq\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathrm{Lag}}(\overline{L}_{\gamma}).

Full normal index does not transfer automatically: a pulled-back
coexact mode acquires profile-dependent weights and derivative energy,
and may cease to be negative.

## 6.4.Two failures of embeddedness

## Example 6.13(Loss of embeddedness under Hopf projection).

Letf1=f2=ff_{1}=f_{2}=fbe the phase-normalized canonical lift of the Clifford torus𝕋2⊂ℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{T}^{2}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}on its threefold holonomy cover:f​(u,v)=13​(ei​u,ei​v,e−i​(u+v))f(u,v)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(e^{iu},e^{iv},e^{-i(u+v)}), with𝖧f=𝖹3\mathsf{H}_{f}=\mathsf{Z}_{3}.
Thusp=q=3p=q=3.
Sinceπ/3<2​π/5<π/3\pi/3<2\pi/5<\pi/\sqrt{3},
symmetric contact monotonicity gives a unique regular profile with(Δ1,Δ2)=(2​π/5,−2​π/5)(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})=(2\pi/5,-2\pi/5).
Asgcd⁡(3,5)=1\gcd(3,5)=1, its primitive spherical quotient is an embedded
special Legendrian in𝕊11\mathbb{S}^{11}, andmG=mγ=N=5m_{G}=m_{\gamma}=N=5.

For the Hopf image inℂ​ℙ5\mathbb{CP}^{5},𝖪12=𝖣12=𝖹3\mathsf{K}_{12}=\mathsf{D}_{12}=\mathsf{Z}_{3},Λ=2​π/3\Lambda=2\pi/3, and(Δ1−Δ2)/Λ=6/5(\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2})/\Lambda=6/5;
hence(A,N)=(6,5)(A,N)=(6,5).
ByTheorem˜6.5,
the projective image has exactly2525clean self-intersection44-tori.
Along each torus exactly two local sheets meet cleanly in codimension
one.
Moreover,Theorems˜6.11and6.12giveIndHam⁡(L¯γ)≥8\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathrm{Ham}}(\overline{L}_{\gamma})\geq 8andIndLag⁡(L¯γ)≥13\operatorname{Ind}_{\mathrm{Lag}}(\overline{L}_{\gamma})\geq 13.
In fact, the symmetric range1<δ/Λ<31<\delta/\Lambda<\sqrt{3}shows thatA=1A=1never occurs for this pair of factors.

Phase saturation plays an important role in the spherical embedding
mechanism: it turns pointwise factor-phase incidences into global
factor returns, as the following example shows.

## Example 6.14(Loss of embeddedness at the spherical product stage).

On the contact levelC1=−1C_{1}=-1, the real-factor construction from a
point and the standard real circle gives a compact connected embedded
special Legendrian torusL⊂𝕊5L\subset\mathbb{S}^{5}.
As constructed inSection˜C.2,
it is not phase-saturated:
there arez±∈Lz_{\pm}\in Landei​ϑ∗∉𝖧Le^{i\vartheta_{*}}\notin\mathsf{H}_{L}such thatz+=ei​ϑ∗​z−z_{+}=e^{i\vartheta_{*}}z_{-}ande3​i​ϑ∗∉{1,−1}e^{3i\vartheta_{*}}\notin\{1,-1\}.

Taking two copies ofLLand the outer steady contact profile,
again onC1=−1C_{1}=-1, gives, after a constant phase rotation,
a compact special Legendrian immersionF:𝕊1×L×L→𝕊11F:\mathbb{S}^{1}\times L\times L\to\mathbb{S}^{11}of the formF​(ϑ,z1,z2)=12​ei​η​(ei​ϑ​z1,e−i​ϑ​z2)F(\vartheta,z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}e^{i\eta}(e^{i\vartheta}z_{1},e^{-i\vartheta}z_{2})

for some constantη\eta.
However,F​(0,z+,z−)=F​(ϑ∗,z−,z+)F(0,z_{+},z_{-})=F(\vartheta_{*},z_{-},z_{+});
the two tangent spaces at the coincidence are distinct.
Hence the outer spiral product is not embedded, although both factors
are embedded special Legendrians.

## Proof.

AppendixC.2constructsLL, proves the nonglobal phase incidence above, and
verifies both the special Legendrian phase ofFFand the
tangent-space distinction.
∎

## Remark 6.15(The two embeddedness tests).

Factor phase saturation, together with the contact
orientation-character argument, controls nonreturn incidences at the
spherical stage; canonical horizontal lifts have this property.
Once the primitive spherical quotient is embedded, its Hopf image is
embedded exactly when the spherical output is phase-saturated,
equivalently, in the present closed contact setting, whenA=1A=1.
Thus input phase saturation does not by itself imply projective
embeddedness.

## Appendix AThreshold and Period Integrals

The coordinate-free Harvey–Lawson continuation inRemark˜2.4already
treats the minimal image through ordinary and collapsed turning slices.
This appendix concerns only the additional parameter-dependent information:
threshold regularity, period and phase returns, endpoint limits, and rank.

## Appendix BQuotient Seams and Cutoff Hessians

This section supplies the seam approximation and cutoff calculations used together with
the Sturm estimates in the quotient index arguments.

## B.1.Transfer through a mapping-torus seam

## Proof ofLemma˜4.4.

Letℰ\mathcal{E}be the normal bundle pulled back to[0,TG]×M1×M2[0,T_{G}]\times M_{1}\times M_{2}.
Zero endpoint traces satisfy the𝒜Φ\mathcal{A}_{\Phi}-twisted seam
condition for every normal-bundle seam isometry.
By trace density, smooth sections supported away from the endpoint
faces are dense inH01​(ℰ)H_{0}^{1}(\mathcal{E}). On a finite-dimensional negative
space this approximation is uniform, so a sufficiently close
smoothing preserves both dimension and strict negativity. Its image
avoids the seam and therefore descends under every seam gluing.
No derivative-trace condition is required at the form level.
∎

We give the remaining incidence and projective-cover calculations used inSections˜5.2.4and6.

connected real-analytic strata[4].
On a stratum𝒮\mathcal{S}, take(i​υ​ζ,A,B)∈T(ζ,a,b)​𝒮(i\upsilon\zeta,A,B)\in T_{(\zeta,a,b)}\mathcal{S}.

Thus the phase projectionΓf→𝕊1\Gamma_{f}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}is constant on each connected stratum, and its image𝒫f\mathcal{P}_{f}is finite.

and setθ^i​(t)=θi​(t)−θi​(0)\widehat{\theta}_{i}(t)=\theta_{i}(t)-\theta_{i}(0).
